Data augmented bootstrap: Unifying confidence interval construction by approximate invariance
Kevin Han Huang

Abstract
We propose the data augmented bootstrap (DAB), a framework for constructing confidence intervals from approximately invariant transformations of the data. As special cases, DAB recovers popular methods that rely on exact group symmetries, such as conformal prediction, wild bootstrap for Maximum Mean Discrepancy U-statistics and the recently proposed SymmPI. Meanwhile, DAB also recovers the classical bootstrap method, which exploits the dataset’s approximate invariance under uniform sampling of data indices as the dataset size grows. For all DAB methods, we establish theoretical coverage results that interpolate between finite-sample and asymptotic guarantees according to the strength of the invariance, and without assuming a group structure. The approximate invariance is measured in the Kolmogorov distance and, for statistics that satisfy Gaussian universality, reduces to conditional mean and variance matching. This allows us to incorporate data augmentation (DA), a widely used machine learning heuristic based on approximate invariances, into known statistical methods. We empirically test the performance of incorporating DA into bootstrap, wild bootstrap and conformal prediction for simulated settings as well as for image, language and scientific data.



\settocbibname

References

1 Introduction

Many methods now exist for constructing confidence intervals based on certain group invariance structure of the underlying data. A very popular example is conformal prediction
[
86
]
: Roughly speaking, a confidence interval (CI) for the
(n+1)
-th output
Y_{n+1}
for some input
X_{n+1}
is produced by ranking

\displaystyle R_{1},\,R_{2},\ldots,\,R_{n+1}\;,

where
(R_{i})_{i\leq n+1}
are residuals for the i.i.d. input-output pairs
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\leq n}
; see
1
among others for detailed reviews. The validity of conformal prediction, at least in its simplest form, rests on the exchangeability of
(R_{i})_{i\leq n+1}
, which implies

\displaystyle R_{i}\;\overset{d}{=}\;R_{j}\qquad\text{ for all }1\leq i,j\leq n+1\;.

A similar invariance structure has been used in other setups: In permutation tests (see e.g.
34
), one considers test statistics that are distributionally invariant under data index permutations; in wild bootstrap methods for kernel two-sample tests, one can simulate certain degenerate U-statistics by exploiting their invariance under reweighting with Rademacher variables
[
78
]
; a recent work
[
23
]
extends conformal prediction to general group symmetries, and shows that adding invariances improves performance. The guarantees for these methods all rest on the
exact distributional invariance
of a test statistic
f(X)
under some group transformations
\Phi_{b}
:

\displaystyle f(X)\;\overset{d}{=}\;f(\Phi_{b}(X))\;,\qquad\text{ dataset }\;\;X\coloneqq(X_{i})_{i\leq n}\;.

(1)

Under (
1
), all such methods enjoy distribution-free, finite-sample validity, i.e. their CIs have the correct coverage for every
n
(up to an
O(\frac{1}{n})
error) and do not require users to know the distribution of
X
. However, the requirement of exact group invariance can be too stringent: It excludes many practically relevant notions of approximate invariance, such as small-angle random rotations of images, random shuffling of text data, and other data augmentation
1
1

1

We follow the convention in machine learning and use
data augmentation
to mean synthetic transformations of a dataset. This differs from a separate meaning in statistics, i.e. the addition of latent variables.
methods from machine learning
[
79
,
80
,
15
,
44
]
. Moreover, the size of the CI, which measures its efficiency, does depend on the distribution of
X
. For conformal prediction, tremendous efforts have been invested in designing problem-specific score functions (i.e. design of the residual
R_{i}
’s) to obtain efficient CIs
[
56
,
73
,
75
,
74
,
50
]
.

To motivate how approximate invariance may be incorporated, we turn to the classical method of bootstrap
[
26
]
, whose link to exchangeability was already noted by
70
. Our key observation is that bootstrap operates under a suitable notion of approximate invariance. At a high level, the bootstrap empirical distribution is formed by ranking

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}X_{i}\,,\,\quad\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}X_{\pi_{1i}}\,,\,\quad\ldots\,,\,\quad\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}X_{\pi_{Bi}}\;,

where
(\pi_{bi})_{b\leq B,i\leq n}
are i.i.d. uniform samples from the index set
[n]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,n\}
. If we view
f(X)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}X_{i}
and
f(\Phi_{b}(X))=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}X_{\pi_{bi}}
, the transformation
\Phi_{b}
replaces the data indices
\{1,\ldots,n\}
with a uniformly draw element of the
n
-fold product
[n]^{n}
, and can be identified as a uniformly random permutation on
[n]^{n}
. The classical guarantee for bootstrap can be expressed informally as the distributional approximation

\displaystyle f(\Phi_{b}(X))\,|\,X\;\overset{d}{\approx}\;f(X^{\prime})\qquad\text{ as }\;\;n\rightarrow\infty\;,

(2)

where
X^{\prime}
is an i.i.d. copy of the dataset
X
. Comparing (
2
) to (
1
), we see that the validity of bootstrap comes from a notion of approximate invariance:
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}X_{\pi_{bi}}
does not have the same marginal distribution as
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}X_{i}
at finite
n
, but the distributions become close asymptotically. In fact, (
2
) provides a stronger statement due to the conditioning: While
f(\Phi_{b}(X))
and
f(X)
are dependent at finite
n
, they asymptotically decouple.

Section

3.1

exact invariance

Generalising the above observations, this article proposes a framework for constructing CIs that are valid under both exact group invariance and approximate non-group invariance. We coin this framework
data augmented bootstrap
(DAB), due to the theoretical inspiration from bootstrap and the practical motivation of incorporating data augmentations from machine learning.
Table

1
lists known inference methods that fall under DAB. We show that, under this unifying perspective, one may share and extend theoretical insights and practical techniques across the entire DAB family. A key implication is that practical data augmentations, which often only satisfy approximate invariance and may not come from a group structure, can be used in conjunction with existing methods.

Connecting (
2
) to Gaussian universality.
To establish the validity of DAB based on approximate invariance, we require distributional approximation of the form (
2
), which may appear difficult to verify at first sight. This article establishes (
2
) for general classes of estimators and transformations by drawing on the growing body of work on Gaussian universality
[
66
,
11
,
51
,
65
,
41
]
. Gaussian universality is the observation that, for many functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{nd}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and a suitably behaved
\mathbb{R}^{nd}
-valued random vector
X
,

\displaystyle f(X)\;\overset{d}{\approx}\;f(Z)\;,\qquad\text{ where }Z\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{E}[X],\text{\rm Var}[X])\;,

(3)

and the distributional approximation becomes exact as
n,d\rightarrow\infty
. When (
3
) holds, the asymptotic distribution of
f(X)
is completely characterised by the mean and variance of
X
. For i.i.d.
X_{i}
’s, Gaussian universality has been observed for a large class of estimators found in high-dimensional statistics and machine learning (e.g.
65
,
41
,
32
and other works discussed in
Appendix

B
, and in
44
,
27
for dependent data under data augmentations). The most relevant work to our setup is
3
, who rigorously proves Gaussian universality for the bootstrap method and establishes
conditional
approximations of the form (
2
) for a general
f
. Our key theoretical tool extends their result to a general class of estimators under the dependence structure induced by DAB; the full universality result is found in
Appendix

E
and the implied coverage guarantee is found in
Theorem

5
in the main text.

Theorem

2
: Coverage under
approx. exchangeability
of
(f(X),f(\Phi_{1}(X)),\cdots,f(\Phi_{B}(X)))

Theorem

3
: Coverage under

approx. invariance
:

f(\Phi_{1}(X))|X\approx f(X)
in
d_{\rm Kol}

Theorem

5
(also
Theorem

16
in the appendix): Coverage under

\bullet

approx. mean & variance matching
:

\|\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]-\mathbb{E}[X]\|_{\infty}
,
\|\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]-\text{\rm Var}[X]\|_{\infty}
small

\bullet

universality conditions

e.g. stability & local dependence

Figure 1
:
Illustration of our theoretical results.

The article is organised as follows:
Section

2
defines DAB.
Section

3
establishes theoretical coverage results under exact and approximate invariance, with guarantees interpolating between finite-sample and asymptotic validity;
Figure

1
provides an overview. No group assumption is required, and in the case of approximate invariance, we also show how Gaussian universality simplifies the approximation.
Section

4
empirically studies the performance of DAB by comparing it against vanilla bootstrap, wild bootstrap for kernel-based U-statistics and conformal prediction, and across simulated and real-life data.
Section

5
concludes by discussing the efficiency of DAB, connections to invariance tests, open problems and comparison to a concurrent work
[
67
]
. The supplement
includes additional examples (
Appendix

A
), further literature comparisons (
Appendix

B
), proofs (
Appendices

C
,
D
,
E
and
F
) and experiment details (
Appendix

H
).

1.1. Notation

Data.

X=(X_{\rm obs},X_{\rm unobs})
is a random dataset formed by concatenating a sequence of random observations
X_{\rm obs}
and a sequence of unknown random variables
X_{\rm unobs}
, which are possibly dependent.
\mathcal{X}_{n}
denotes a generic measurable space that
X
takes value in, and
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm unobs}
denotes that of
X_{\rm unobs}
. For the universality result only, we require
X
to live in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{nd}
, and will assume
X=(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
is a set of
n
random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that are possibly dependent. Throughout,
\mathbb{X}=\sigma(X)
denotes the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X
, and similarly
{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}=\sigma(X_{\rm obs})
. We also write
\mathbb{A}_{\rm trivial}
as the trivial
\sigma
-algebra.

Transformations.
Let
\mathbb{T}
be the set of all
\mathcal{X}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{n}
functions, i.e. its elements operate on the entire dataset. For
1\leq b\leq B
,
\Phi_{b},\Psi_{b}
are taken to be random elements of
\mathbb{T}
.
\mathbb{T}
is not assumed to be a group in general, and its elements can be non-invertible. Deterministic transformations, such as the cycling operation used in conformal prediction, are represented by taking
\Phi_{b},\Psi_{b}
’s as some almost surely deterministic functions.

Statistic.
We consider a general statistic
f:\mathcal{X}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, where
\mathcal{Y}
is some space equipped with a total order.
\mathcal{Y}
is used whenever no assumption other than the total order is made, and we make it clear that
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}
when the restriction is necessary.

Index sets.
We write
[B]=\{1,\ldots,B\}
and
[B]_{0}=\{0\}\cup[B]
.

Ranking variables.
In conformal prediction, the prediction interval is formed by ranking the residuals. Similarly in DAB, we need a suitable notion of ranking. We allow a general ranking function
{\rm Rank}_{T}:\mathcal{Y}^{B+1}\rightarrow[0,B+1]
, defined based on the total order of
\mathcal{Y}
as

\displaystyle{\rm Rank}_{T}\big(v;(v_{b})_{b\in[B]}\big)\coloneqq\msum_{b=1}^{B}\mathbb{I}_{\{v>v_{b}\}}+T\big(\msum_{b=1}^{B}\mathbb{I}_{\{v=v_{b}\}}+1\big)\;,

where
T
is a random
\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
tie-breaking function. Common choices are:

\bullet

Uniform tie-breaking.

T(n)\sim\text{Uniform}(\{0,1,\ldots,n\})
;

\bullet

Smoothed uniform tie-breaking.

T(n)\sim\text{Uniform}([0,n])
;

In conformal prediction, uniform tie-breaking can incur an
O(\frac{1}{B})
coverage error, whereas smoothed uniform tie-breaking gives exact coverage (Chapter 2,
86
).

2 Data augmented bootstrap

Given a dataset of observed and unobserved random variables
X=(X_{\rm obs},X_{\rm unobs})
, a central task of statistics is to provide a valid and efficient coverage for properties of
X_{\rm unobs}
. More formally, for
\alpha\in[0,1]
, we seek a random confidence region
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{\rm unobs}\in\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(X_{\rm obs}))\;\geq\;1-\alpha\;.

Data augmented bootstrap (DAB) is a family of methods of constructing
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
by approximating a statistic
f(X)
by suitably transformed versions of the statistic. Consider
f:\mathcal{X}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and a set of
B
possibly random transformations
\Phi\coloneqq(\Phi_{b})_{b\leq B}
. For
\alpha\in[0,1]
, let
S_{\alpha}
be a size-
(1-\alpha)
subset of
[0,1]
. The DAB prediction set is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}}\coloneqq\Big\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm unobs}\;\Big|\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\Rank_T\big( \, f( X_{\rm obs}, \tilde\bx_n) \,;\, (f(\Phi_b(X_{\rm obs}, \tilde\bx_n)))_{b=1}^B \, \big)}{B+1}$}}\,\in\,S_{\alpha}\Big\}\;.

(6)

Informally, DAB operates under the assumption that

\displaystyle\big(\,f(X)\,,\,f(\Phi_{1}(X))\,,\,\ldots\,,\,f(\Phi_{B}(X))\,\big)\quad\text{ is approximately exchangeable\,,}

since when exchangeability holds, an appropriately chosen and normalised rank function of the
B+1
variables above will be distributed as
U\sim{\rm Uniform}[0,1]
, in which case

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{\rm unobs}\in{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}}(X_{\rm obs}))\;\approx\;{\mathbb{P}}(U\in S_{\alpha})\;=\;1-\alpha\;.

Here,
{\rm Uniform}[0,1]
plays the role of a pivotal distribution in the sense of
37
: The rank variable is an appropriate transformation of our statistics such that its distribution becomes independent of data and allows for the construction of a valid CI.

Example 1

(Bootstrap for 1d mean estimation)
.

Let
\tilde{X}=(\tilde{X}_{i})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d. 1d variables with unknown
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}_{1}]
to be estimated (viewed as part of
X_{\rm unobs}
) and unit variance. To see how bootstrap is a form of DAB, we identify the data
X=(\tilde{X}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{n},\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}_{1}])\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\equiv\mathcal{X}_{n}
, the statistic
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},x_{*})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{*})
, and the transformations

\displaystyle\Phi_{b}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},x_{*})\;=\;(x_{\pi_{b1}}\,,\,\ldots\,,\,x_{\pi_{bn}}\,,\,\bar{x})\;,\quad\bar{x}=\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}x_{i}\;.

where
(\pi_{bi})_{b\leq B,i\leq n}
are i.i.d. uniform draws from
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Choosing
S_{\alpha}=(\frac{\alpha}{2},1-\frac{\alpha}{2})
, the DAB confidence interval
{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}}(\tilde{X})
for
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}_{1}]
is given by

\displaystyle\Big[\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}\tilde{X}_{i}-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\hat{q}^{(B)}_{1-\alpha/2}\;,\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}\tilde{X}_{i}-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\hat{q}^{(B)}_{\alpha/2}\Big]

where
\hat{q}_{\omega}^{(B)}
denotes the
\omega
-th quantile of the empirical distribution

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{B+1}$}}\Big(\msum_{b\leq B}\delta_{f(\Phi_{b}(X))}+\delta_{f(X)}\Big)\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{B+1}$}}\Big(\msum_{b\leq B}\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}(X_{\pi_{bi}}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\leq n}X_{j})}+\delta_{f(X)}\Big)\;.

i.e. the bootstrap distribution plus an extra point mass at
f(X)
. For
B
large,
{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}}(\tilde{X})
gives the nonparametric bootstrap CI for
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}_{1}]
. Similarly, setting
\hat{s}_{x}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}
and
(\eta_{bi})_{b\leq B,i\leq n}
as i.i.d. standard normals, the parametric bootstrap CI can be obtained from

\displaystyle\Phi_{b}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},x_{*})\;=\;(n^{-1/2}\hat{s}_{x}\,\eta_{b1}\,,\,\ldots\,,\,n^{-1/2}\hat{s}_{x}\,\eta_{bn}\,,\,0)\;.

Remark 1

(Alternative coverage statements)
.

Our theoretical results present coverage statements of the form (
) for simplicity. In practice, instead of a confidence region for the unobserved data
X_{\rm unobs}
, it may be desirable to ask for the following:

(i)
Confidence region for some downstream property
\rho(X)
of
X
.
The coverage guarantee (
) for
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
is still relevant, as it implies a coverage guarantee for the induced confidence region
\rho(\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},X_{\rm obs})\coloneqq\{\rho(X_{\rm obs},\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n})\,|\,\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(X_{\rm obs})\}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big(\rho(X)\in\rho(\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},X_{\rm obs})\big)\;\geq\;{\mathbb{P}}(X_{\rm unobs}\in\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(X_{\rm obs}))\;\overset{\eqref{eq:C:valid}}{\geq}\;1-\alpha\;.

(ii)
Conditional coverage.
It is often desirable to obtain guarantees that hold conditionally on the observed information, represented via the
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}=\sigma(X_{\rm obs})
as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{\rm unobs}\in\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(X_{\rm obs})\,|\,{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}})\geq 1-\alpha\qquad\text{ almost surely\,.}

Our theoretical results can be applied to obtain conditional coverage, provided that their corresponding assumptions are replaced by the conditional versions given
{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}
; see
Remarks

2
and
4
, the discussion and examples in
Section

3.2
and the proof of all results in the appendix. Note that for conformal prediction, impossibility results on conditional coverage are well-established
[
87
,
57
,
29
]
, and DAB does not alleviate this issue since our assumptions do not generally hold conditionally for conformal prediction.

3 Coverage results

This section presents the theoretical results for DAB. We first obtain coverage guarantees under an approximate exchangeability condition on
f(X)
,
f(\Phi_{1}(X))
,
\ldots
,
f(\Phi_{B}(X))
(
Section

3.1
). When
(\Phi_{b})_{b\leq B}
are i.i.d., we show that this approximate exchangeability can be quantified by a suitable conditional notion of approximate invariance on
f(X)
under
\Phi_{b}
(
Section

3.2
). By exploiting a Gaussian universality assumption, this approximate invariance can be further reduced to differences in only the first two moments (
Section

3.3
). Each of the results generalise known guarantees for existing DAB methods, as we shall illustrate in various examples. Some key differences of our results are: (i) Group assumption is not required; (ii) Additional approximate invariances can be incorporated into known methods; (iii) They naturally interpolate between finite-sample guarantees under exact group invariance and asymptotic guarantees under approximate invariance.

3.1. Validity under approximate exchangeability

DAB involves ranking the sequence
(f(X),f(\Phi_{1}(X)),\ldots,f(\Phi_{B}(X)))
. To define approximate exchangeability, we compare this sequence to an exactly exchangeable one:

Definition 1

(Exactly exchangeable surrogate)
.

Let
\Psi_{0},\ldots,\Psi_{B}
be
B+1
random elements of
\mathbb{T}
such that
(f(\Psi_{0}(X)),f(\Psi_{1}(X)),\ldots,f(\Psi_{B}(X)))
is exchangeable.

Consider the rank variable
R_{\Phi}(X)\coloneqq{\rm Rank}_{T}\big(f(X)\,;\,(f(\Phi_{b}(X)))_{b\in[B]}\big)
used in DAB and the analogue
R_{\Psi}(X)\coloneqq{\rm Rank}_{T}\big(f(\Psi_{0}(X))\,;\,(f(\Psi_{b}(X)))_{b\in[B]}\big)
for the exchangeable surrogate. We measure approximate exchangeability by the c.d.f. difference

\displaystyle\Delta^{r}_{\rm Kol}\;\coloneqq\;\big|\,{\mathbb{P}}\big(R_{\Phi}(X)/(B+1)<r\big)\,-\,

\displaystyle\,{\mathbb{P}}\big(R_{\Psi}(X)/(B+1)<r\big)\,\big|\qquad\text{ for }r\in[0,1]\;.

Observe that with the same notation, the coverage probability for the DAB CI,
{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}}
, reads

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big(X_{\rm unobs}\in{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}}(X_{\rm obs})\big)\;=\;{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R_\Phi(X)}{B+1}$}}\,\in\,S_{\alpha}\Big)\;.

We now control this probability under approximate exchangeability and for two common

tie-breaking choices. See
Section

C.1
for the result for general tie-breaking functions.

Theorem 2
.

Let
r\in[0,1]
. For uniform tie-breaking,

\displaystyle\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R_{\Phi}(X)}{B+1}$}}\,<\,r\Big)-r\Big|\;\leq\;\Delta^{r}_{\rm Kol}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{B+1}$}}\;,

and for smoothed uniform tie-breaking,

\displaystyle\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R_{\Phi}(X)}{B+1}$}}\,<\,r\Big)-r\Big|\;\leq\;\Delta^{r}_{\rm Kol}\;.

Remark 2

(Conditional coverage)
.

{\mathbb{P}}
can be replaced by
{\mathbb{P}}({\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}|{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}})
if the exchangeability condition in
Section

3.1
holds conditionally on
{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}
.

Theorem

2
recovers known coverage guarantees under group invariance. To see this, let
\mathbb{G}
be a compact group with
X\overset{d}{=}g(X)
for all
g\in\mathbb{G}
. If either (i)
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{B}\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\textrm{Uniform}(\mathbb{G})
or (ii)
\mathbb{G}
is finite with size
B+1
,
\Phi_{0}={\rm id}
and
\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{B}
is a deterministic enumeration of the non-identity elements of
\mathbb{G}
, then exactly exchangeability holds for

\displaystyle\big(X,\Phi_{1}(X),\ldots,\Phi_{B}(X)\big)\overset{d}{=}

\displaystyle\;\big(\Phi_{0}(X),\Phi_{1}\Phi_{0}(X),\ldots,\Phi_{B}\Phi_{0}(X)\big)\;.

(8)

In this case,
\Delta^{r}_{\rm Kol}=0
for all
r\in[0,1]
, and
Theorem

2
implies
(1-\alpha)
coverage (up to an
O(\frac{1}{B})
error) for any size-
(1-\alpha)
interval
S_{\alpha}\subseteq[0,1]
. The same group invariance and corresponding guarantees can be found in conformal prediction with
\mathbb{G}
as the cyclic group, permutation tests with the permutation group, SymmPI with a general compact group
[
23
]
, and the wild bootstrap procedures discussed next.

Example 2

(Exact coverage for wild bootstrap for U-statistics)
.

Let
X=(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d. mean-zero random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f(X)=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{\top}X_{j}
. The wild bootstrap procedure used for simulating degenerate U-statistics
[
22
,
30
,
59
,
19
]
is a special case of DAB by drawing i.i.d. Rademacher variables
(\epsilon_{bi})_{b\leq B,i\leq n}
and identifying

\displaystyle\Phi_{b}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\;\coloneqq\;(\epsilon_{b1}x_{1}\,,\,\ldots\,,\,\epsilon_{bn}x_{n})\;.

(9)

Assume sign-flipping symmetry
X_{1}\overset{d}{=}-X_{1}
; this arises, for example, in the Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) statistic in two-sample tests (see
Section

4.2
). Then

\displaystyle f(\Phi_{b}(X))\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n(n-1)}$}}\msum_{i\neq j}\epsilon_{bi}\epsilon_{bj}X_{i}^{\top}X_{j}\;\overset{d}{=}\;f(X)\;.

Exact exchangeability holds by identifying the group
\mathbb{G}=\{-1,+1\}^{n}
in the argument in (
8
), and
Theorem

2
holds with
\Delta^{r}_{\rm Kol}=0
. Note that this argument also generalises beyond a linear dot product kernel. This gives the same finite-sample coverage for wild bootstrap for MMD by
78
, who first exploited the sign-flipping invariance.

When
\Delta^{r}_{\rm Kol}>0
,
Theorem

2
provides coverage beyond the exact group invariance discussed above. For conformal prediction, prior works
[
5
,
90
]
have measured the errors of exchangeability violation in the total variation or Wasserstein distance. We will see that our c.d.f. difference allows us to connect approximate invariance to universality results typically established in Kolmogorov distance.

Remark 3
.

A well-studied violation of
Section

3.1
is weighted exchangeability. For conformal predictions and permutation tests, it has been shown
[
84
,
5
,
71
]
that coverage can still be obtained by using suitable reweightings; see
Appendix

B
for a further literature review and discussion. We expect that with a similar reweighted rank function as
71
, one may also obtain coverage for DAB by approximating
(f(X),f(\Phi_{1}(X)),\ldots,f(\Phi_{B}(X)))
with a weighted exchangeable sequence.

3.2. Validity under approximate invariance

The approximate exchangeability error
\Delta^{r}_{\rm Kol}
involves identifying an exchangeable surrogate sequence of statistics, which is compared against the original sequence. We now show that this error can be reduced to a suitable notion of approximate invariance of the original statistic
f(X)
under the transformation
\Phi_{1}
. We use a simplifying assumption:

Assumption 1
.

(i)
\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{B}
are i.i.d. random elements of
\mathbb{T}
and independent of
X
;
(ii) smoothed uniform tie-breaking is used;
(iii) the output space of
f
,
\mathcal{Y}
, is taken to be
\mathbb{R}
.

The idea is to apply
Theorem

2
with some appropriate
\Psi_{b}
’s, and control the error via

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\;\coloneqq\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\Phi_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|\;.

\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
compares the conditional distribution of
f(\Phi_{1}(X))|X
to that of
f(X)
through a Kolmogorov distance. The coverage statement is stated in terms of a control of
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
in the
L_{\nu}
norm,
\|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}\|_{L_{\nu}}\coloneqq(\mathbb{E}[|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}|^{\nu}])^{1/\nu}
, defined for
\nu\geq 1
.

Theorem 3
.

Let
r\in[0,1]
,
\nu\geq 1
and
B\geq 2
. Under Assumption
1
, almost surely

\displaystyle\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R_\Phi(X)}{B+1}$}}<r\Big)\,-\,r\Big|\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{12}{4^{1/(\nu+1)}}$}}\,\big(3\,\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{\nu}}\big)^{\frac{\nu}{\nu+1}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{B-1}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sqrt{3 \log B}}{\sqrt{B-1}}$}}\;.

Remark 4

(Conditional coverage)
.

To control
{\mathbb{P}}(\frac{R_{\Phi}(X)}{B+1}<r\,|\,{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}})
, it suffices to replace
{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)
in the definition of
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
by
{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t\,|\,{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}})
and the
L_{\nu}
norm by its conditional analogue
(\mathbb{E}[|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}|^{\nu}\,|\,{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}])^{1/\nu}
; see
Example

5
in the appendix and
Appendix

D
.

Remark 5
.

The first term in
Theorem

3
adapts to the type of control we have on
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
: If we only have
L_{1}
-control, the first term reads
6\sqrt{3}\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|^{1/2}_{L_{1}}
; if
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\leq\bar{\Delta}
almost surely for some deterministic
\bar{\Delta}
, taking
\nu\rightarrow\infty
gives
36\bar{\Delta}
; if
X=(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
is a set of
n
observations and
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then
\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{\nu}}\rightarrow 0
since
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
is almost surely bounded, and we obtain asymptotic coverage.

\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
differs from the typical notion of approximate invariance, as it compares the
conditional
distribution
f(\Phi_{1}(X))|X
rather than the marginal distribution
f(\Phi_{1}(X))
to the distribution of
f(X)
. The next example shows how this approximate invariance arises in the classical guarantees for bootstrap, and illustrates how
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
can be controlled.

Example 3

(Asymptotic coverage for bootstrap)
.

Consider the nonparametric bootstrap setting in
Example

1
. A classical guarantee of bootstrap
[
26
,
37
]
can be stated as follows: Let
(\tilde{X}^{\prime}_{i})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d. copies of
\tilde{X}_{1}
. Under mild conditions, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, almost surely

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\,\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sum_{i=1}^n (\tilde X_{\pi_{1i}} - \frac{1}{n} \sum_{j \leq n} \tilde X_j) }{\sqrt{n}}$}}\leq t\,\Big|\,X\Big)-{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sum_{i=1}^n (\tilde X_i' - \mean[\tilde X_1]) }{\sqrt{n}}$}}\leq t\Big)\Big|\rightarrow 0\;.

With the notation of
Example

1
, this is exactly the statement that, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)=\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\Phi_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)-{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|\rightarrow 0\;\text{ almost surely},

(10)

which implies asymptotic coverage of bootstrap by
Theorem

3
. This is a case of
asymptotic invariance
: At finite
n
, even the marginal distribution of
f(\Phi_{1}(X))
disagrees with
f(X)
(consider the “bad” event when all
(\pi_{bi})_{i\leq n}
select the same data point), but the large-
n
asymptotic drives the probability of the “bad” events to zero and thus
f(\Phi_{1}(X))|X
and
f(X)
to the same normal distribution. Meanwhile, the conditioning on
X
ensures that under (
10
),
f(\Phi_{1}(X))|X
and
f(X)
are asymptotically independent. This makes
(f(X),f(\Phi_{1}(X)),\ldots,f(\Phi_{B}(X)))
asymptotically i.i.d. and thus exchangeable.

1
(i) excludes transformations
\Phi^{\prime}=(\Phi^{\prime}_{b})_{b\leq B}
that are a deterministic enumeration of the elements from a finite set, such as the cycling operations in conformal prediction and Jackknife. The next lemma shows that
\Phi^{\prime}
is equivalent to uniform random sampling, making
Theorem

3
still applicable. Again, we do not assume a group structure.

Lemma 4
.

Let
r\in[0,1]
,
\nu\geq 1
,
\mathcal{S}_{B}=(\Phi^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Phi^{\prime}_{B})
be a deterministic subset of
\mathbb{T}
and
(\Phi_{b})_{b\leq B}
be i.i.d. uniform draws from
\mathcal{S}_{B}
. Under
1
(ii) and (iii),

\displaystyle\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R_{\Phi'}(X)}{B+1}$}}<r\Big)-{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R_{\Phi}(X)}{B+1}$}}<r\Big)\Big|\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{4 \sqrt{\log(2B)}}{\sqrt{2B}}$}}\,+\,5\,\big(3\big\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\big\|_{L_{\nu}}\big)^{\nu/(\nu+1)}\;,

where we have denoted
R_{\Phi^{\prime}}(X)\coloneqq{\rm Rank}_{T}\big(f(X)\,;\,(f(\Phi^{\prime}_{b}(X)))_{b\in[B]}\big)
.

Remark 6
.

For
\nu=1
, the error in
Section

3.2
is
O(\frac{\sqrt{\log B}}{\sqrt{B}}+\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})
, which is worse than the error in
Theorem

3
. This is because our proof uses the concentration of
\frac{R_{\Phi}(X)}{B+1}
around the conditional mean
\mathbb{E}[\frac{R_{\Phi}(X)}{B+1}|X,T]=\frac{R_{\Phi^{\prime}}(X)}{B+1}
, which incurs an
B^{-\frac{1}{2}}
error.

Appendix

A
contains additional examples of
Theorem

3
, including asymptotic coverage for wild bootstrap (
Example

4
) and Jackknife (
Example

7
), and conditional asymptotic coverage for simple cases of bootstrap (
Example

5
) and permutation test (
Example

6
).
Section

D.1
also includes a generalisation of
Theorem

3
when, instead of
1
(i),
\Phi_{b}
’s are generated conditionally i.i.d. given some generic
\sigma
-algebra
\mathbb{A}
.

3.3. Quantifying approximate invariance via Gaussian universality

Example

3
shows how
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
may be controlled for empirical averages, but many practical examples involve highly non-linear
f
’s. One way to control
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
for a more general
f
is via Gaussian universality: If we can replace
\Phi_{1}(X)|X
and
X
by appropriately chosen Gaussians,
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
may be measured through only moment differences in the data. We now restrict the input space to
\mathcal{X}_{n}=(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}
.
n
is the number of data points and
d\equiv d(n)
is the data dimension, which is allowed to grow with
n
:

\displaystyle d\,/\,n\;\rightarrow

\displaystyle\;\gamma\qquad\text{ for some }\gamma\in[0,\infty]\;.

(11)

We will use Gaussian universality to control
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
via the variance difference

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)\;\coloneqq\;\|\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]-\text{\rm Var}[X]\|_{\infty}\;,

where
\|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}\|_{\infty}
is the infinity norm on
\mathbb{R}^{nd\times nd}
. The standard ingredients in Gaussian universality are (i) a matching mean condition; (ii) a dependency condition on the data; (iii) a stability condition on
f
; (iv) a moment boundedness condition. We now formulate these for DAB. Since we can always WLOG absorb the mean and variance of
X
into
f
by considering the transformed statistic
\boldsymbol{x}\mapsto f(\text{\rm Var}[X]^{1/2}\boldsymbol{x}+\mathbb{E}[X])
, we first assume:

Assumption 2

(Standardisation)
.

\mathbb{E}[X]=0
and
\text{\rm Var}[X]=I_{nd}
.

In the main text, we suppose that
\Phi_{1}
is chosen such that the conditional mean of
\Phi_{1}(X)
matches that of
X
. This holds, for example, in the case of bootstrap in
Example

1
.

Assumption 3

(Exactly matching conditional mean)
.

\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]=\mathbb{E}[X]
almost surely.

Since
X
may consist of dependent coordinates (despite
2
) and the transformation
\Phi_{1}
can introduce dependence, we need to employ a universality result under local dependence. For an
\mathbb{R}^{nd}
-valued random vector
V
distributed according to
\mu
, we denote the local dependency neighbourhood of the
(i,j)
-th coordinate
V_{ij}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{ij}(\mu)\coloneqq\inf\big\{\mathcal{N}\subseteq[n]\times[d]\;\big|\;(i,j)\in\mathcal{N}\,,\,(V_{ab})_{(a,b)\in\mathcal{N}}\text{ is independent of }(V_{ab})_{(a,b)\not\in\mathcal{N}}\big\}.

We denote the maximum size of the local dependency neighbourhood of
V
as

\displaystyle N(\mu)\;\coloneqq\;\max_{1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq j\leq d}\,\big|\mathcal{N}_{ij}(\mu)\big|\;.

(12)

Note that
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(\mu)
and
N(\mu)
are deterministic quantities. Let
\mu_{\Phi_{1}(X)|X}
be the conditional probability measure for
\Phi_{1}(X)|X
, whose existence is assumed throughout this paper (see
28
), and
\mu_{X}
be the probability measure for
X
. We assume the following:

Assumption 4

(Local dependency)
.

N_{\rm dep}\coloneqq\max\{N(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(X)|X})\,,\,N(\mu_{X})\}
is
o((nd)^{\frac{1}{4}})
.

Let
X^{\prime}
be an i.i.d. copy of
X
. We seek to use Gaussian universality to approximate
\Phi_{1}(X)
, conditioning on
X
, by a Gaussian vector
Z^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{nd})
, and then approximate
Z^{\prime}
by
X^{\prime}
. The approximations are valid under stability conditions on
f
, assumed here to be thrice-differentiable. For
\tau=1,2,3
and
\nu\geq 1
, we measure the stability of
f
along a set of suitable interpolation paths between the variables
\Phi_{1}(X)
,
Z^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle\varphi_{\tau;\nu}\;\coloneqq\;(nd)^{\frac{\tau}{2}}\,\sup_{i\leq n,\,j\leq d\,,\,\gamma\in\mathcal{C}}\,\Big\|\,\big\|\partial_{(i,j)}^{\tau}\,f\big(\gamma(\Phi_{1}(X),X^{\prime},Z^{\prime})\big)\big\|_{L_{4}|X}\,\Big\|_{L_{\nu}}\;.

(13)

\partial^{\tau}_{(i,j)}
is the
\tau
-th partial derivative with respect to the
j
-th coordinate of the
i
-th observation, and
\|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}\|_{L_{4}|X}\coloneqq(\mathbb{E}[|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}|^{4}|X])^{\frac{1}{4}}
.
(nd)^{\frac{\tau}{2}}
balances typical scaling factors obtained from differentiating the statistic
f
(consider, for instance, the sample average
\frac{1}{\sqrt{nd}}\sum_{i\leq n,j\leq d}x_{ij}
).
\mathcal{C}
is a set of
(\mathbb{R}^{nd})^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{nd}
functions that represent the interpolation paths, defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\;\coloneqq\;\big\{\gamma_{s,\theta;i,j}\,,\,\gamma^{\prime}_{s,\theta;i,j}\big\}_{s,\,\theta\in[0,1]\,;\,i\leq n,\,j\leq d}\;,

where, for
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}
, the
(a,b)
-th coordinate of each interpolation path is given as

\displaystyle\big(\gamma_{s,\theta;i,j}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})\big)_{ab}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\begin{cases}\theta\big(\sqrt{s}\,x_{ab}\,+\,\sqrt{1-s}\,z^{\prime}_{ab}\big)\;,&\text{ if }\;(a,b)\in\mathcal{N}_{ij}(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(X)|X})\;,\\
\sqrt{s}\,x_{ab}\,+\,\sqrt{1-s}\,z^{\prime}_{ab}\;,&\text{ if }\;(a,b)\not\in\mathcal{N}_{ij}(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(X)|X})\;,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\big(\gamma^{\prime}_{s,\theta;i,j}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})\big)_{ab}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\begin{cases}\theta\big(\sqrt{s}\,x^{\prime}_{ab}\,+\,\sqrt{1-s}\,z^{\prime}_{ab}\big)\;,&\text{ if }\;(a,b)\in\mathcal{N}_{ij}(\mu_{X})\;,\\
\sqrt{s}\,x^{\prime}_{ab}\,+\,\sqrt{1-s}\,z^{\prime}_{ab}\;,&\text{ if }\;(a,b)\not\in\mathcal{N}_{ij}(\mu_{X})\;.\end{cases}

See
Section

E.1
for more detailed interpretations. The stability terms can depend on
N_{\rm dep}
, which may grow with
n
; the next condition ensures that it does not grow too fast.

Assumption 5

(Stability)
.

There exists some
\nu\geq 1
such that, for
\tau=1,2,3
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\tau;(7-\tau)\nu}\;=\;o\Big(\min\Big\{\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{nd}{N_{\rm dep}^4}$}}\Big)^{1/(2\tau)}\,,\,(N_{\rm dep}\,\|\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)\|_{L_{3\nu/2}})^{-\frac{1}{\tau}}\Big\}\Big)\;.

We also require a moment boundedness condition:

Assumption 6

(Moment boundedness)
.

For
\nu
given in
5
, we have

\displaystyle\max\nolimits_{i\leq n,j\leq d}\,\max\big\{\big\|\,\|(\Phi_{1}(X))_{ij}\|_{L_{4}|X}\,\big\|_{L_{6\nu}}\,,\,\|X_{ij}\|_{L_{4}}\big\}\,\;=\;O(1)\;.

The next condition is stated for convenience and relaxed in the proof in
Appendix

E
.

Assumption 7

(Density smoothness)
.

The random variable
f(Z^{\prime})
has a Lebesgue density that is uniformly bounded from above by some constant that does not depend on
n
or
d
.

Under
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
and
7
, we can use Gaussian universality to replace
\Phi_{1}(X)|X
and
X
by the corresponding (conditional) Gaussians, and subsequently control
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
through a variance difference
\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)=\|\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]-\text{\rm Var}[X]\|_{\infty}
. We state the asymptotic result here, and refer readers to
Appendix

E
for a finite-sample bound.

Theorem 5
.

Under
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
and
7
and the asymptotic (
11
), with
\nu\geq 1
given in
5
, if additionally
\|\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}=o(N_{\rm dep}^{-1})
, then

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{\nu}}\;\rightarrow\;0\;.

(14)

If additionally
1
holds, for
B
allowed to be fixed or growing with
n
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,1]}\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R_\Phi(X)}{B+1}$}}<r\Big)\,-\,r\Big|\;\rightarrow\;0\;.

(15)

The condition on
\|\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}
is satisfied for
\|\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}=o((nd)^{-\frac{1}{4}})
under
4
. (
14
) is proved by an interpolation method standard in universality results
[
65
,
27
]
, and (
15
) follows from (
14
). The conditions of
Theorem

5
can be difficult to parse for readers unfamiliar with the universality literature. We make several comments:

(a)
4
,
5
and
6
(local dependency, stability and moment boundedness) are standard in the universality literature; see prior works referenced in
Section

1
and
Section

E.1
for a detailed discussion of these conditions. We verify local dependency for different transformations
\Phi_{1}
in
Section

E.3
. We also discuss the applicability of stability for examples of
f
in
Section

E.4
, such as estimators that can be approximated by a delta method, U-statistics in wild bootstrap and conformal prediction score functions;

(b)
2
and
7
appear to be restrictive but can be relaxed, as discussed earlier;

(c)    The strongest condition is the matching mean condition in
3
. In
Remark

7
in
Section

E.1
, we show that a mean mismatch can be accommodated at the expense of more notation and a first-order stability condition. We also show that, while the
\|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}\|_{\infty}
norm on
\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)
can introduce additional dependence on
n
and
d
, this can be replaced by a quantity that measures the average coordinate of the matrix
\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)
;

(d)   While
Theorem

5
requires conditional moment matching, we discuss in
Appendix

G
how these can be reduced to marginal moment matching in specific examples.

Theorem

5
says that, under Gaussian universality, coverage can be established if the transformation
\Phi_{1}
“reproduce the first two moments of the original data”. For bootstrap of the 1d empirical average (
Example

1
),
\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]=\mathbb{E}[X]=0
, whereas
\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]
is a diagonal
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
matrix with equal entries on the diagonal,

\displaystyle(\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X])_{11}\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}\Big(X_{i}-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{j\leq n}X_{j}\Big)^{2}\;,

which is a consistent estimator of
\text{\rm Var}[X_{1}]
. In particular,

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}\;=\;\big\|\,|(\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X])_{11}-\text{\rm Var}[X_{1}]|\,\big\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}\;=\;O(n^{-1/2})\;\rightarrow\;0\;,

so
Theorem

5
holds. Indeed, bootstrap consistency for the empirical average relies on the CLT, which is a special case of Gaussian universality. For bootstrap on a general function
f
,
3
use Gaussian universality to obtain finite-sample bounds for a quantity similar to
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
. They observe that, without knowledge of the true mean
\mathbb{E}[X]
, the bootstrapped mean and the data mean are typically mismatched, and the error gets amplified when
f
is unstable (see their Example 3.4 and Section 3.2), making a first-order stability condition necessary for bootstrap consistency. We make the same observation in
Remark

7
in
Section

E.1
. Thus, an alternative way to view
Theorem

5
is that it extends the coverage guarantees for bootstrap to more general DAB confidence intervals, constructed with other approximately invariant transformations.

3.4. Some useful extensions of
Theorems

3
and
5

Simplified variance-matching criterion.

Theorem

5
aims to cover a large class of statistics and, at the cost of its generality, requires that
\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]
needs to (asymptotically) match
\text{\rm Var}[X]
uniformly across all observations and coordinates. This can be relaxed for statistics with more structures. For instance, consider a plug-in estimator

\displaystyle f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\;=\;g\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}x_{i}\Big)

(16)

where
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function. Provided that
\partial g(\mathbb{E}[X_{1}])\neq 0
and under regularity conditions, for
d
fixed, the CLT and the delta method imply that

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sqrt{n}}{ \sqrt{ (\partial g(\mean[X_1]))^\top\Var[X_1] \, \partial g(\mean[X_1])} }$}}\,(f(X)-g(\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]))\;\xrightarrow{d}\;\mathcal{N}(0,1)\qquad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty\;.

For i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
transformations
\phi_{bi}
’s, we consider

\displaystyle\;\hskip 95.00014pt\Phi_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\;=\;(\phi_{11}(x_{1}),\ldots,\phi_{1n}(x_{n}))\;,

(17)

\displaystyle\;\mu_{\phi}(X)\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}\mathbb{E}[\phi_{11}(X_{i})|X_{i}]\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Sigma_{\phi}(X)\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}\text{\rm Var}[\phi_{11}(X_{i})|X_{i}]\;.

By a similar argument with the central limit theorem for independent but non-identically distributed random variables, and under additional regularity conditions on
\phi_{bi}
’s, the following conditional Kolmogorov distance converges to zero almost surely:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sqrt{n} \, ( f(\Phi_1(X)) - g(\mu_\phi(X)) ) }{((\partial g(\mu_\phi(X)))^\top\Sigma_\phi(X) \, \partial g(\mu_\phi(X)) )^{1/2}}$}}\leq t\,\Big|\,X\Big)-{\mathbb{P}}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}(Z\leq t)\Big|.

Then, instead of needing to match
(\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X],\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X])
with
(\mathbb{E}[X],\text{\rm Var}[X])
uniformly across coordinates, it suffices to match the empirical averages

\displaystyle(\mu_{\phi}(X),\Sigma_{\phi}(X))\;\approx\;(\mathbb{E}[X_{1}],\text{\rm Var}[X_{1}])\;.

(18)

This also works for a growing
d
by the non-classical delta method in
45
.

Composing approximately invariant transformations with exactly invariant transformations

In practice, one may wish to incorporate approximately invariant transformations
(\tilde{\Phi}_{b})_{b\leq B}
into a DAB method whose original transformations
(\mathcal{T}_{b})_{b\leq B}
already enjoy exchangeability. Naively applying
Theorem

3
, the error
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
would be measured with respect to the composite transformation
\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
. By adapting the proof of
Theorem

3
, we show that only the invariance error with respect to
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
is required:

Lemma 6
.

Suppose
\Phi_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
are independent random elements of
\mathbb{T}
that are independent of
X
. Assume that
\mathcal{T}_{1}(X)
has the same marginal distribution as
X
. Then, denoting the random function
f_{\mathcal{T}}\coloneqq f\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}
, we have that almost surely

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X,f_{\mathcal{T}})\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\mathcal{T}}(X)\leq t\,|\,f_{\mathcal{T}})\big|\,\Big|\,X\Big]\;.

If instead
\Phi_{1}=\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}
, for
\nu\geq 1
, almost surely

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{\nu}}\;\leq\;\big\|\,\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|\;\big\|_{L_{\nu}}\;.

4 New DAB methods by incorporating approximately invariant transformations

For simplicity, let
X=(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{d}
random vectors and
(\Phi_{b})_{b\leq B}
be i.i.d. drawn unless otherwise specified. The coverage results in
Section

3
enable us to build DAB methods from approximately invariant transformations. In this section, we empirically investigate observation-wise data augmentations and bootstrap (
Section

4.1
), wild bootstrap (
Section

4.2
) and conformal prediction (
Section

4.3
). In particular, for known DAB methods with transformations
(\Phi^{\rm old}_{b})_{b\leq B}
— i.e. bootstrap, wild bootstrap and conformal predictions — we introduce new transformations
(\Phi^{\rm new}_{b})_{b\leq B}
by taking the composition

\displaystyle\Phi_{b}\;=\;\Phi^{\rm old}_{b}\circ\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}\;.

For each example, we derive the invariance conditions required for coverage in
Appendix

G
. Broadly speaking, for those in
Sections

4.1
and
4.2
, Gaussian universality applies and we only need conditional moment matching, which can be relaxed to marginal moment matching for bootstrap. For the ones in
Section

4.3
, we show that it suffices to have
marginal
c.d.f. matching between
f(\Phi_{b}(X))
and
f(X)
, which is a relaxation of the requirement in
Section

3.2
.

4.1. Observation-wise transformations and variants of bootstrap

Let
\phi_{bi}
’s be i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
transformations. We consider observation-wise transforms

\displaystyle\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\coloneqq(\phi_{b1}(x_{1}),\ldots,\phi_{bn}(x_{n}))\;.

(19)

This is exactly
data augmentations
in the machine learning literature
[
79
,
80
,
15
,
44
]
. We consider two DAB confidence intervals (CIs): (i) DAB with
\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}
, i.e. purely observation-wise data augmentations; (ii) DAB with
\Phi^{\rm bootstrap}_{b}\circ\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}
, where
\Phi^{\rm bootstrap}_{b}
are the bootstrap transformations defined in
Example

1
. Two concrete examples are considered:

Orthogonal transformations for mean-zero and isotropic random vectors.
Consider the toy setting where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{d}
random vectors with
d
fixed, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]=0\;,\;\;\text{\rm Var}[X_{1}]=I_{d}\;\;\text{ and }\;\;f(X)=\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}X_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{1}_{d},\;\;\;\text{ where }\boldsymbol{1}_{d}=(1,\ldots,1)^{\top}.

Let
\mathbb{O}_{d}
be the group of
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
orthogonal matrices, and draw the augmentations in (
19
) as

\displaystyle\phi_{11},\ldots,\phi_{Bn}\;\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\;\textrm{Uniform}(\mathbb{O}_{d})\;.

(20)

Neither exchangeability nor group invariance is guaranteed in this case, since
X_{i}
’s may not be Gaussian. Nevertheless, asymptotic validity holds under Gaussian universality.

Statistics on parallel sampling in AI-for-science.
In modern multi-electron simulations, one models the joint distribution of electron positions in a physical system by some large neural network
p_{\hat{\theta}}

[
9
,
40
,
68
]
. The true distribution
p_{*}
typically satisfies invariance with respect to a group
\mathbb{G}
,

\displaystyle X^{*}\overset{d}{=}g(X^{*})\quad\text{ for all }g\in\mathbb{G}\text{ and }X^{*}\sim p_{\theta^{*}}\;,

(21)

but the trained network may not
[
46
]
. To assess the quality of
p_{\hat{\theta}}
, one runs
n
parallel, independent MCMC chains targeting
p_{\hat{\theta}}
. At step
t
, this yields
n
i.i.d. samples
X^{(t)}\coloneqq(X^{(t)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(t)}_{n})
, where
X^{(t)}_{i}\sim q_{t}
and
q_{t}
approximates
p_{\hat{\theta}}
, and one estimates

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X\sim p_{\hat{\theta}}}[h(X)]\qquad\text{ by }\qquad f(X^{(t)})\coloneqq\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}h\big(X^{(t)}_{i}\big)

(22)

where
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a function of interest. A confidence interval for (
22
) helps to understand whether sufficiently many chains have been used, and is typically quantified by Gaussian and bootstrap CIs. In view of the invariance (
21
), a natural idea is to incorporate transformations from
\mathbb{G}
. Specifically, we draw the augmentations in (
19
) as

\displaystyle\phi_{11},\ldots,\phi_{Bn}\;\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\;\textrm{Uniform}(\mathbb{G})\;.

Note again that invariance is not guaranteed at a finite step
t
.

For both setups, there are now four candidate CIs: the Gaussian CI, the DAB CI with
\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}
, the bootstrap CI with
\Phi^{\rm bootstrap}
and the DAB CI with
\Phi^{\rm bootstrap}\circ\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}
.
Figure

2
reports simulations for
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{2})
, where orthogonal transformations
\mathbb{O}_{2}
are used for
\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}
. We find that (i) all CIs have valid coverage and similar sizes at
n=30
, when the CLT approximates an i.i.d. average of
n
random variables well; (ii) at a smaller sample size
n=5
,
\mathbb{O}_{2}
transformations improve coverage. The same empirical findings hold for non-Gaussian random vectors, in which case only approximate invariance holds; see
Section

H.1
.
Figure

3
confirms the findings for samples from AI-for-science neural nets and with a suitably chosen rotational group
\mathbb{G}
; see
Section

H.1
for the full setup.

.

for plots with varying

.

4.2. Wild bootstrap with additional transformations for degenerate U-statistics

Given i.i.d. samples
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
from a distribution
P
in
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
and i.i.d. samples
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
from a distribution
Q
, we consider testing
H_{0}:P=Q
with Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD)
[
35
]
. We follow
83
to define the kernel function
\kappa(y,y^{\prime})\coloneqq\langle\varphi(y),\varphi(y^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
for feature map
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
and a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. The MMD is defined as

D(Q,P)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{Y,Y^{\prime}\sim P}[\kappa(Y,Y^{\prime})]-2\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P,Z\sim Q}[\kappa(Y,Z)]+\mathbb{E}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\sim Q}[\kappa(Z,Z^{\prime})]
,
and a standard unbiased estimator is the degree-two U-statistic

\displaystyle D_{n}\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n(n-1)}$}}\msum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\,u(Y_{i},Y_{j},Z_{i},Z_{j})\;,

where
u(y,y^{\prime},z,z^{\prime})\coloneqq\kappa(y,y^{\prime})+\kappa(z,z^{\prime})-\kappa(y,z^{\prime})-\kappa(y^{\prime},z)
. Under
H_{0}
,
D(Q,P)=0
, and
D_{n}
is degenerate and asymptotically distributed as an infinite sum of weighted chi-squares, which is intractable to simulate. Wild bootstrap, as illustrated in
Example

2
, provides a finite-sample-valid CI for
D_{n}
by exploiting sign-flipping symmetry.

Suppose we know that
P
is approximately invariant under some random
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
transformation
\phi
. Let
(\phi_{ri})_{r\in\{1,2\},i\leq n}
be i.i.d. copies of
\phi
and
\epsilon_{i}
’s be i.i.d. Rademacher random variables in (
9
). We propose DAB with the transformed statistic

\displaystyle f(\Phi_{1}(X))\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n(n-1)}$}}\msum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{j}\,u\big(\,\phi_{1i}(Y_{i}),\phi_{1j}(Y_{j}),\phi_{2i}(Z_{i}),\phi_{2j}(Z_{j})\big)\;.

(23)

Section

G.2
shows that for valid coverage under
H_{0}
, the approximate invariance condition can be formulated via conditional moment matching, but in the space of the feature map
\varphi
rather than in the original data space. While this conditional moment matching condition is unverifiable for kernels beyond simple linear kernels such as
\kappa(y,y^{\prime})=y^{\top}y^{\prime}
, we empirically compare the DAB method (
23
) against wild bootstrap under four settings, all with the Gaussian RBF kernel: (a) 2d simulated data under mean shift (
Figure

4
); (b) Noiseless v.s. noisy MNIST images (
Figure

5
); (c) odd v.s. even-numbered classes in CIFAR-10 images (
Section

H.2
); (d) physical signal v.s. noise in a HIGGS boson dataset (
Section

H.2
). Our DAB variant visibly improves test power in (a), (b) and (c), and has a similar test power as vanilla wild bootstrap in (d).

,

and over 1000 random draws of the dataset, with a fixed target Type-I error

4.3. Conformal prediction with data augmentation

Consider i.i.d. random input-output pairs
X_{i}=(V_{i},Y_{i})
, such that
(X_{i})_{2\leq i\leq n}
is the calibration set and
X_{1}
is the new data point. To see that split conformal prediction (CP) is an example of DAB, we identify
(\Phi_{b})_{b\leq B}
with
B=n-1
as an enumeration of all deterministic cycling operations on the
n
input-output pairs,
(x_{i})_{i\leq n}=(v_{i},y_{i})_{i\leq n}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\Phi^{\rm CP}_{b}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\;=\;\big(x_{\sigma_{b}(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma_{b}(n)})\;,\quad\sigma_{b}(i)\coloneqq((i+b-1)\;{\rm mod}\;n)+1\;.

Then, for some score function
h:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm out}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm out}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and some estimator
g:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm in}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm out}}
fitted on a hold-out dataset, we can identify the statistic in DAB as the conformity score

\displaystyle f(X)\;=\;h(Y_{1},\,g(V_{1}))\;,\qquad\text{ which implies }\qquad f\big(\Phi^{\rm CP}_{b}(X)\big)\;=\;h(Y_{b+1},\,g(V_{b+1}))\;.

We again assume approximate invariance under a random transformation
\phi=(\phi^{\rm in},\phi^{\rm out})
, and form DAB from
(n-1)k
transformations: Let
\phi_{bji}
be i.i.d. copies of
\phi
and define

\displaystyle\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{bj}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\Phi^{\rm CP}_{b}\big(\phi_{bj1}(x_{1})\,,\,\ldots\,,\,\phi_{bjn}(x_{n})\big)\;\;\text{for }1\leq b\leq n-1\text{ and }1\leq j\leq k.

Unlike the earlier examples, universality no longer holds:
f(X)
is completely determined by
X_{1}
, and violates the stability condition required for
Theorem

5
. Nonetheless, we still have guarantees from the conditional c.d.f. matching in
Theorem

3
which, for
k=1
, can be improved to marginal c.d.f. matching (
Section

G.3
) measured by

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm CP}_{\rm inv}\;\coloneqq\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}\big(h(\phi^{\rm out}(Y_{1}),\,g(\phi^{\rm in}(V_{1})))\leq t\big)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}\big(h(Y_{1},\,g(V_{1}))\leq t\big)\big|

\sigma=0
(ii)

:

500
random trials and for
1-\alpha=95\%
. (i) In each trial, a random
\beta\in\mathcal{N}(0,I_{2})
is drawn and a size-
n
dataset of
X_{i}=(V_{i},Y_{i})
is generated as
Y_{i}=V_{i}^{\top}\beta+\epsilon_{i}
, where
V_{i}
is coordinate-wise i.i.d. centred Gamma variables with shape and rate
1
, and
\epsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
0.8n
data is used for training and
0.2n
for calibration. The DAB CI is formed with
k=50
for predicting the outcome
Y_{\rm new}
of a fresh i.i.d. drawn
V_{\rm new}
. (ii) We split the set of C4h-symmetric molecules in the QM-sym dataset of
60
into two halves, one for training the GMP+SNN neural network used in
42
, and the other for calibration. The DAB CI is formed with
k=1
for predicting the energy of a randomly chosen molecule. The
x
-axis indicates the GPU seconds required to generate the CI per random trial, whereas the number next to each data point indicates the size of the calibration set used.

Figure

7
compares DAB to CP in two setups: (a) For linear regression on simulated data, DAB has better coverage at small
n
but is more conservative than CP at large
n
. (b) For neural networks on molecular data, we use the CP score function proposed by
42
, which involves computing the distance of the input molecule to the
K
closest neighbours in the training data. This computation can be costly, as it involves ordering the entire training set by distance from the input molecule. Our DAB variant uses a randomized selection of
K
training points instead (denoted
Randomized Neighbours
in
Figure

7
(ii)), and gives a smaller CI with a small coverage loss within a given amount of compute.
Section

H.3
includes setup details and further experiments with large language models, where DAB shows similar performance as vanilla CP.

5 Discussion

The key idea behind DAB is the following: While group symmetries are mathematically elegant as a definition of invariance, for statistical desiderata such as coverage of a confidence interval (CI), we can afford a looser interpretation of invariance —
that a transformed statistic has a similar distribution as that of the original statistic. DAB unifies two prevalent families of methods for confidence interval constructions: One that is based on exact group invariance and provides black-box, finite-sample guarantees, and one that is based on asymptotic theories and provides estimator-specific but asymptotically exact guarantees. This provides the ground for incorporating data augmentations, a technique ubiquitous in machine learning (ML) for incorporating approximate and non-group invariances, into uncertainty quantification procedures. In various ML domains, tremendous empirical efforts have been spent on optimising the augmentation choice to improve the accuracy of estimators
[
79
,
80
]
; DAB opens the possibilities for doing the same for improving the quality of CIs. Our empirical results are a first step in this direction: We see visible improvements with DAB in many tasks, but also limited gains when not many symmetries are available (Higgs boson and text examples,
Appendix

H
). Many interesting questions are open as to what transformations are optimal for each task, and the answers are likely to be domain-specific.

A crucial aspect of confidence intervals is their efficiency. Under the well-known duality of a CI and a hypothesis test
[
55
]
, the efficiency of the DAB CI
C_{\alpha}(X_{\rm obs})
is the power of its induced test statistic
\mathbb{I}\{{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}\not\in C_{\alpha}(X_{\rm obs})\}
for testing whether some given random variable
W
follows the same distribution as
X_{\rm unobs}
. In fact, the indistinguishable hypotheses for DAB are exactly the distributions that satisfy the same invariances built into DAB. As such, DAB can be viewed as a test statistic for an invariance test
[
55
,
24
,
18
,
17
]
. The efficiency question about DAB becomes a question about the test power of DAB against a broad class of non-invariant alternatives, and our work can be viewed as providing Type-I error control and empirical investigations on the Type-II error. An interesting future direction is to make the connection to testing rigorous and obtain the worst-case test power guarantee of DAB under different alternatives.

One of our key tools is Gaussian universality, which substitutes the role of classical CLT in bootstrap and extends DAB to estimators that are not asymptotically normal. The core observation is that, to obtain coverage guarantees for DAB, it suffices to establish that the distributions of two suitably chosen statistics are close, regardless of whether the intermediate distributional approximation simplifies to a tractable one e.g. Gaussian. Similarly, there is no reason to restrict ourselves to estimators whose intermediate approximations are described by Gaussian universality. By exploiting modern distributional approximation results for heavy-tailed data and random matrices, we conjecture that DAB can be extended to a much wider range of settings than those considered in this article. In particular, the finite-sample bounds of our
Theorems

2
and
3
provide an entry point for any quantitative estimates for suitable distributional approximation results to be plugged in and yield coverage guarantees. Moreover, the Euclidean structures assumed in
Theorems

3
and
5
are specific to our usage of the Kolmogorov metric and Gaussian universality tools. We conjecture that non-Euclidean variants of DAB can be obtained by adapting our proofs to a different metric with suitable non-Euclidean limit theorems.

After the release of an initial preprint, we were alerted of a concurrent work
[
67
]
that independently notice the connection between conformal prediction and bootstrap. They obtain results similar to our
Theorems

2
and
3
but also notably accommodating a non-uniform pivot and non-identically distributed variables to be ranked. As applications, they study a range of statistical estimators in detail, and show that asymptotically valid CIs can be obtained without infinitely many resampling steps. In contrast, our emphasis is on the interpretation via invariances and the incorporation of data augmentations, with theoretical groundings from Gaussian universality results on dependent data (
Theorem

5
) and empirical validation with augmentations from ML settings (
Section

4
). At a technical level,
67
consider CIs without tie-breaking and impose a continuity condition (through a Lévy concentration function); we do not impose this due to our use of smooth tie-breaking. We expect that their results can be combined with our universality results to expand the scope of DAB.

The author thanks Peter Orbanz and Vasco Portilheiro for the helpful discussions and support in this work. The author also thanks Antonin Schrab for helpful comments, especially on wild bootstrap, and for suggesting the name of data augmented bootstrap (DAB). We are grateful to Arun Kumar Kuchibhotla, Woonyoung Chang and Manit Paul for pointing out the connection to
67
and an issue with an earlier version of
Theorem

3
. All codes can be found at
github.com/KevHH/DAB_code
. The codes for Higgs boson, QM-sym and language data have been assisted by Codex.

Supplementary materials to “Data augmented bootstrap: Unifying confidence interval construction by approximate invariance”

The supplementary materials are organised as follows:

Appendix

A
provides additional examples of data augmented bootstrap (DAB).

Appendix

B
provides additional discussions on related works.

Appendix

C
provides additional results and proofs for the approximate exchangeability results in
Section

3.1
.

Appendix

D
provides additional results and proofs for the approximate invariance results in
Section

3.2
.

Appendix

E
states and proves additional Gaussian universality results that complement
Section

3.3
, and verifies universality for specific examples.

Appendix

F
proves
Section

3.4
, which concerns the composition of approximately invariant transformations and exactly invariant ones.

Appendix

G
derives the theoretical conditions for coverage guarantees to hold for each example considered in
Section

4
.

Appendix

H
includes experimental details and additional empirical results that complement those reported in
Section

4
.

Appendix A
Additional examples of DAB

Example 4

(Asymptotic coverage for wild bootstrap)
.

Let
X
and
f(X)
be defined as in
Example

2
with the symmetry
X_{1}\overset{d}{=}-X_{1}
. We consider a more general setup of wild bootstrap, where
\Phi_{b}
is defined as in
Example

2
but with generic i.i.d. variables
(\epsilon_{bi})_{b\leq B,i\leq n}
with zero mean and unit variance. Note that
\Phi_{b}
does not come from a group structure in general, e.g. in the case of
\epsilon_{bi}
’s being standard Gaussians. A classical result of
22
, in the case
d=1
and the U-statistic
f(X)
is degenerate, gives that

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{2}}\;=\;\big\|\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\Phi_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)-{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|\big\|_{L_{2}}\rightarrow 0\;.

By
Theorem

3
, this again implies valid coverage for wild bootstrap. Similar guarantees are given in
48
for non-degenerate U-statistics, in which case non-negative weights
(\epsilon_{bi})_{b\leq B,i\leq n}
are used.

Example 5

(Conditional coverage for bootstrap)
.

We inherit the notation from
Example

3
, and consider conditioning on
{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}=\sigma(\tilde{X}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{m})
for some
m<n
. Recall that
\text{\rm Var}[\tilde{X}_{1}]=1
and assume for simplicity that
\mathbb{E}|\tilde{X}_{1}|^{4}<\infty
. Then the Berry-Esséen bound applies: Almost surely,

\displaystyle\;\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\,\Big|\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t\,|\,{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}})-{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sqrt{n-m} \, \eta+ \sum_{i=1}^m (\tilde X_i - \mean[\tilde X_1]) }{\sqrt{n}}$}}\leq t\,\Big|\,{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}\Big)\Big|\,\leq\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \mean|\tilde X_1|^3 }{\sqrt{n-m}}$}}\,,

where
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is independent of
{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(\Phi_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{\sigma}\eta\leq t\,|\,X)\Big|\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\hat\kappa_3}{\sqrt{n} \, \hat\sigma^3}$}}\qquad\text{ almost surely, }

where
\hat{\sigma}^{2}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\leq n}\tilde{X}_{j})^{2}
and
\hat{\kappa}_{3}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}|\tilde{X}_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\leq n}\tilde{X}_{j}|^{3}
. Meanwhile by the Markov inequality, for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big|\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sum_{i=1}^m (\tilde X_i - \mean[\tilde X_1])}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\Big|>\epsilon\Big)=

\displaystyle\,O\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m}{n \epsilon^2}$}}\Big)\;,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big|\hat{\sigma}^{2}-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(n-m) \Var[\tilde X_1] }{n}$}}\Big|>\epsilon\Big)=

\displaystyle\,O\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n (\epsilon+ \frac{n}{m})^2}$}}\Big)

Therefore, provided that
m=o(n)
, we have that as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\;=\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\Phi_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)-{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t\,|\,{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}})\big|\;=\;o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\;,

where
o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
denotes convergence to zero in probability over the distribution of
X
. By
Theorem

3
and
Remark

5
, we obtain valid conditional coverage for bootstrap given
(\tilde{X}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{m})
provided that
m=o(n)
.

Example 6

(Conditional coverage for a permutation test)
.

Consider a stylised setup of testing the difference of two distributions
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, given their respective i.i.d. datasets
(X_{1,i})_{i\leq n}
and
(X_{2,i})_{i\leq n}
, by a permutation test. This can be identified as DAB in (
6
) with
X=(X_{1,1},\ldots,X_{2,n})
,
f(X)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\msum_{i\leq n}\kappa(X_{1,i},X_{2,i})
for some kernel function
\kappa:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and, for
1\leq b\leq B
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{b}(x_{1,1},\ldots,x_{2,n})\;=\;(x_{\pi_{b}(1,1)},\ldots,x_{\pi_{b}(2,n)})\;,

where
\pi_{b}
’s are uniformly drawn permutations on the index set
\{(1,1),\ldots,(2,n)\}
. Under the null hypothesis
P_{1}=P_{2}
,
(f(X),f(\Phi_{1}(X)),\ldots,f(\Phi_{B}(X)))
is marginally exchangeable, so
Theorem

2
implies exact marginal coverage. However, this exchangeability is generally violated when we condition on part of the dataset. On the other hand, assuming mild moment conditions on
\kappa(X_{11},X_{21})
under
P_{1}=P_{2}
, the same argument as
Example

5
implies that asymptotic conditional guarantee holds with high probability, if the conditioning is on
\sigma(X_{11},\ldots,X_{2m})
for
m=o(n)
. As a consequence, one may choose the kernel function
\kappa
based on the observations
X_{11},\ldots,X_{2m}
.

Example 7

(Coverage for leave-one-out methods)
.

Consider a set
X=(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\leq n+1}
of i.i.d. covariate-label pairs in
(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})^{n+1}
. Let
g:(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a regressor such that
g((x_{i},y_{i})_{i\leq n},x_{0})
gives the prediction on
x_{0}
by the regressor trained on
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i\leq n}
and
g
is invariant under permutations of the
n-1
training data pairs. We also use
*
to denote missing data and use
g((*,*),(x_{i},y_{i})_{i\leq n-1},x_{n+1})
to denote a regressor that is only trained on
n-1
data. We consider the leave-one-out method studied in
82
(referred to as the Jackknife method by
4
), and identify the method as DAB (
6
) for predicting
Y_{n+1}
. Let
B=n
and
(\Phi_{b})_{b\leq n}
be a deterministic enumeration induced by cyclic shifts on
[n]
:

\displaystyle\Phi_{b}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},v_{n+1})\;=\;((*,*),v_{(b\textrm{ mod }n)+1},\ldots,v_{(b-2\textrm{ mod }n)+1},v_{b})\;.

The estimator
f
for the leave-one-out method can be identified as

\displaystyle f(X)\;=\;|Y_{n+1}-g((X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\leq n},X_{n+1})|\;.

Exact exchangeability does not hold in this case since, for example,

\displaystyle f(\Phi_{1}(X))\;=\;\big|\,Y_{1}-g((\star,\star),(X_{i},Y_{i})_{2\leq i\leq n},X_{1})\,\big|\;\overset{d}{\neq}\;f(X)\;.

Nevertheless,
82
establishes asymptotic guarantees for stable estimators that, for example, include high-dimensional linear predictors.
82
shows, in a similar style as
Example

1
, that
f(\Phi_{1}(X))|X
and
f(X)
both converge to the same distribution. Under our notation, their result implies

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\Phi_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)-{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|\rightarrow 0\;\;\text{ in probability}\;.

This statement implies asymptotic exchangeability of
(f(X),f(\Phi_{1}(X)),\ldots,f(\Phi_{B}(X)))
— for example, in the sense of
Theorem

3
, with an additional application of
Section

3.2
to connect the deterministic transformations to random transformations — which leads to valid asymptotic coverage. On the other hand,
4
introduces Jackknife+, a modification of the leave-one-out method above, which is also a special case of DAB and makes use of exact exchangeability to obtain finite-sample guarantees similar to
Theorem

2
and the examples discussed after.

Appendix B
An extended discussion of related work

The proposed framework of data augmented bootstrap (DAB) shares connections with a diverse range of literature, and we include a further discussion in this section.

Data augmentation.
Data augmentations have been widely employed in machine learning tasks
[
79
,
80
]
. In terms of theoretical results in statistics,
15
is one of the first theoretical works to study the effect of group-based augmentations on a class of statistical estimators, where
44
is the first to study non-group augmentations without requiring invariance. Notably,
44
also employs Gaussian universality with dependence, and their theoretical results have inspired this work. Yet, both
15
and
44
focus on the effect of data augmentations on estimator quality, and neither has considered its application to uncertainty quantification. SymmPI
[
23
]
makes the novel observation that proof techniques for conformal prediction can be extended to general groups, and applies group-based transformations for CI constructions under the condition of exact invariance or equivariance. However, both the group assumption and the invariance assumption exclude many commonly used augmentations. An interesting recent work
[
89
]
explores incorporating data augmentations for uncertainty quantification via the Bayesian posteriors. While their Bayesian approach is fundamentally different from DAB, an interesting follow-up direction could be exploring how DAB may be combined with Bayesian approaches for uncertainty quantification, especially in domain-specific problems.

Invariance-based inference.
There is already a large body of literature on invariance-based inference and in particular on conformal prediction; a non-exhaustive list of literature has been referenced in
Section

1
. Of particular relevance to our work are those that have considered a violation of exchangeability: For conformal prediction, many works (e.g.
84
,
5
,
71
,
36
,
69
) have considered addressing the violation of exact exchangeability with reweighting techniques, with successful applications to local heterogeneous effects, distributional shifts and many more settings. As discussed in
Remark

3
, an interesting avenue of future work is to explore the combination of DAB and weighted ranks for similar setups. When not enough distributional knowledge is known to design exact and computable weights, the incurred coverage error has been controlled in terms of total variation distance
[
5
]
or Wasserstein distance
[
90
]
. In contrast, our
Theorem

2
has controlled the coverage error through a c.d.f. difference that can be turned into a Kolmogorov distance comparison between
f(\Phi_{1}(X))|X
and
f(X)
. This is what enables Gaussian universality tools to be applied and the connection to bootstrap results to be established. The application of Gaussian universality to compare
f(\Phi_{1}(X))|X
and
f(X)
is also heavily inspired by the Gaussian universality toolkits in
3
, which establishes bootstrap consistency for estimators beyond empirical averages and can be viewed as a special case of our
Theorem

5
.

In the context of permutation tests, the application of asymptotic theory to study coverage validity under the violation of exchangeability is not new: For example,
48
studies this for particular classes of studentised statistics, whereas
7
considers this under approximate group invariance assumptions. In contrast, our results do not assume a group structure, and address a broader class of estimators, i.e. those for which Gaussian universality applies; see a recent work
[
45
]
for a characterisation of such estimators as well as results on when universality fails for high-dimensional data. Meanwhile, as discussed in
Section

5
, we conjecture that the DAB framework can be extended to settings beyond Gaussian universality.

Gaussian universality.
Over the past decade, a wave of results have established Gaussian universality theoretically and empirically for many estimators found in high-dimensional statistics and machine learning, including but not limited to: random feature models
[
41
]
, regularised regression
[
38
]
, generalised linear models
[
20
]
, perceptron models
[
32
]
, max-margin classifiers
[
64
]
and general classes of empirical risk minimisers
[
65
]
; see
47
for a review of the founding probability works in this area as well as recent results. Our universality result adapts the techniques of
27
to accommodate local dependence as well as the conditioning introduced in our measurement of approximation invariance error. A related work
[
33
]
obtains training-conditional coverage of conformal prediction by concentration results related to the training data in the proportional regime. We note that their result addresses a different problem from ours, since our dataset
X
of interest corresponds to the calibration data and the new data point in the context of conformal prediction. In particular, as discussed in
Section

4.3
, our universality result does
not
apply to conformal prediction due to a lack of stability, even though our approximate invariance result does.

Appendix C
Additional results and proofs for
Section

3.1
: Approximate exchangeability

C.1. Coverage result under general tie-breaking function

We provide a generalisation of
Theorem

2
that accommodates arbitrary tie-breaking functions. This requires additional notation, which we state next.

Random quantities to be ranked.
Given a subset
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
, we denote its associated multiset by
\lbag\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\rbag
, where only the unique values in
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
and the associated multiplicities matter. As shorthands, we denote

\displaystyle V\;\coloneqq\;(V_{0},V_{1},\ldots,V_{B})\;,\qquad V_{b}\;\coloneqq\;f(\Psi_{b}(X))\qquad\text{ for }b\in[B]_{0}\;,

which concern the
\mathcal{Y}
-valued random variables to be ranked in the definition of
R_{\Psi}(X)
.
Also write
V^{*}_{0}\leq\ldots\leq V^{*}_{B}
as the order statistics of
V_{0},\ldots,V_{B}
. Note that
(V^{*}_{b})_{b\in[B]_{0}}
is
\lbag V\rbag
-measurable.

Ranks of
(V^{*}_{b})_{b\in[B]_{0}}
to account for ties.
It is convenient to define functions that indicate the range of indices of
V^{*}_{0},\ldots,V^{*}_{B}
that are at a tie. To this end, for any fixed
k\in[B]_{0}
, we consider the random variables

\displaystyle R^{*}_{-}(k)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\min\{b\in[B]_{0}\;|\;V^{*}_{b}=V^{*}_{k}\}\;=\;\msum_{b\in[B]_{0}}\mathbb{I}\{V^{*}_{k}>V^{*}_{b}\}\;,

\displaystyle R^{*}_{+}(k)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\max\{b\in[B]_{0}\;|\;V^{*}_{b}=V^{*}_{k}\}\;=\;\msum_{b\in[B]_{0}}\mathbb{I}\{V^{*}_{k}\geq V^{*}_{b}\}-1\;.

(24)

R^{*}_{-}(k)
can be viewed as a zero-indexed rank of
V^{*}_{k}
among
(V^{*}_{b})_{b\in[B]_{0}}
that breaks ties always by preferring the first variable that occurs at a tie, whereas
R^{*}_{+}(k)
can be viewed as a similar rank except that ties are always broken by preferring the last variable that occurs at a tie.
R^{*}_{-}(k)
and
R^{*}_{+}(k)
are completely determined by
\lbag V\rbag
