(V_{i},\xi_{i})
is independent of
I^{(i)}(s,0)
by construction, we obtain

\displaystyle Q(s)\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{2}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{m}\msum_{j\in\mathcal{M}^{V}_{i}}\mint_{0}^{1}\Big|\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\partial^{2}\gamma_{\delta}\big(I^{(i)}(s,0)\big)\,,\,e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\big\rangle\big]\,\mathbb{E}\big[V_{i}V_{j}\,-\,\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\big]\Big|ds

\displaystyle\;+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{2}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{m}\msum_{j,l\in\mathcal{M}^{V}_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mint_{0}^{1}\Big\langle\partial^{3}\gamma_{\delta}\big(I^{(i)}(s,\theta)\big)\,,\,e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes e_{l}\Big\rangle\,(1-\theta)^{2}\,d\theta

\displaystyle\hskip 110.00017pt\times\big(\sqrt{s}\;V_{j}\,+\,\sqrt{1-s}\;\xi_{j}\big)\big(\sqrt{s}\;V_{l}\,+\,\sqrt{1-s}\;\xi_{l}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 110.00017pt\times\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{V_i}{2\sqrt{s}}$}}-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\xi_i}{2\sqrt{1-s}}$}}\Big)\bigg]\bigg|

\displaystyle\;\overset{(a)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m N}{2}$}}\,\Delta_{2}(\gamma_{\delta})\,\|\text{\rm Var}[V]-I_{m}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\;+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m N^2}{6}$}}\,\Delta_{3}(\gamma_{\delta})\,\max_{i\leq m}\max\{\|V_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3}\,,\,\|\xi_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3}\}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{4}{\sqrt{s}}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{4}{\sqrt{1-s}}$}}\Big)

\displaystyle\;\overset{(b)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m N}{2}$}}\,\Delta_{2}(\gamma_{\delta})\,\|\text{\rm Var}[V]-I_{m}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\;+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m N^2}{6}$}}\,\Delta_{3}(\gamma_{\delta})\,\max_{i\leq m}\max\{\|V_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3},3^{\frac{3}{4}}\}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{s}}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{1-s}}$}}\Big)\;.

In
(a)
, we have used the triangle inequality and Hölder’s inequality, and denoted

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}(\gamma_{\delta})\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\sup_{i,j,l\leq m;\,\theta,s\in[0,1]}\,\big\|\big\langle\partial^{2}\gamma_{\delta}\big(I^{(i)}(s,\theta)\big)\,,\,e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\big\rangle\big\|_{L_{4}}\;,

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}(\gamma_{\delta})\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,l\leq m\\
\theta,s\in[0,1]\end{subarray}}\,\big\|\big\langle\partial^{3}\gamma_{\delta}\big(I^{(i)}(s,\theta)\big)\,,\,e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes e_{l}\big\rangle\big\|_{L_{4}}\;.

In
(b)
, we have used the moment formula of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
to compute
\|\xi_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3}
. Integrating
Q(s)
over
s\in[0,1]
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}[\gamma_{\delta}(V)-\gamma_{\delta}(\xi)]|\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m N}{2}$}}\,\Delta_{2}(\gamma_{\delta})\,\|\text{\rm Var}[V]-I_{m}\|_{\infty}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 m N^2 }{3}$}}\,\Delta_{3}(\gamma_{\delta})\,\max_{i\leq m}\max\{\|V_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3},3^{\frac{3}{4}}\}\;.

Recall that
\gamma_{\delta}=h_{t;\delta}\circ g
, where
h_{t;\delta}
is thrice differentiable with
|\partial^{r}h_{t;\delta}|
uniformly bounded by
\delta^{-r}
for
r=1,2,3
. Also recall that by definition, for
r=1,2,3
,

\displaystyle m^{r/2}\hskip-5.0pt\sup_{i,j\leq m;\,\theta,s\in[0,1]}\,\big\|\,\big\|\partial_{j}^{r}g\big(I^{(i)}(s,\theta)\big)\big\|_{\infty}\,\big\|_{L_{4}}\;\leq\;\varphi_{r}(g)\;.

By the chain rule, we can compute

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}(\gamma_{\delta})\;\leq

\displaystyle\;m^{-1}\,\big(\,\delta^{-2}\,(\varphi_{1}(g))^{2}\,+\,\delta^{-1}\,\varphi_{2}(g)\big)\;,

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}(\gamma_{\delta})\;\leq

\displaystyle\;m^{-3/2}\,\big(\,\delta^{-3}\,(\varphi_{1}(g))^{3}\,+\,3\delta^{-2}\,\varphi_{1}(g)\,\varphi_{2}(g)\,+\,\delta^{-1}\,\varphi_{3}(g)\big)\;.

This implies

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}[\gamma_{\delta}(V)-\gamma_{\delta}(\xi)]|\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{N}{2}$}}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(\varphi_1(g))^2}{\delta^2}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\varphi_2(g)}{\delta}$}}\Big)\,\|\text{\rm Var}[V]-I_{m}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\;+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 N^2}{3 \sqrt{m}}$}}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(\varphi_1(g))^3}{\delta^3}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{3 \varphi_1(g) \, \varphi_2(g)}{\delta^2}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\varphi_3(g)}{\delta}$}}\Big)

\displaystyle\;\hskip 10.00002pt\,\times\,\max_{i\leq m}\max\big\{\|V_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3},3^{\frac{3}{4}}\big\}\;.

Recalling that
\gamma_{\delta}=h_{t;\delta}\circ g
and taking a supremum over
t\in\mathbb{R}
proves the desired bound.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

14
.

Proof of
Theorem

14
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\delta>0
and
h_{t;\delta}
be given as in
Section

E.1
with
\tilde{m}=3
. By
Section

E.1
, we can write

\displaystyle\;|{\mathbb{P}}(g(V)>t)-{\mathbb{P}}(g(W)>t)|

\displaystyle\;=\;\max\{{\mathbb{P}}(g(V)>t)-{\mathbb{P}}(g(W)>t)\,,\,{\mathbb{P}}(g(W)>t)-{\mathbb{P}}(g(V)>t)\}

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\max\big\{\mathbb{E}[h_{t;\delta}(g(V))-h_{t+\delta;\delta}(g(W))]\,,\,\mathbb{E}[h_{t;\delta}(g(W))-h_{t+\delta;\delta}(g(V))]\big\}

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{E}[h_{t;\delta}(g(V))-h_{t;\delta}(g(\xi))]\big|+\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{E}[h_{t;\delta}(g(W))-h_{t;\delta}(g(\xi))]\big|

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{|\mathbb{E}[h_{t;\delta}(g(\xi))-h_{t+\delta;\delta}(g(\xi))]|}_{\eqqcolon\,(\star)}\;.

The first two quantities are controlled by
Section

E.1
, whereas by
Section

E.1
again,

\displaystyle(\star)\;\leq\;\big|{\mathbb{P}}(g(\xi)>t-\delta)-{\mathbb{P}}(g(\xi)>t+\delta)\big|\;\leq\;{\mathbb{P}}(|g(\xi)-t|\leq\delta)\;.

Combining the bounds, taking an infimum over
\delta>0
and recalling that
\text{\rm Var}[W]=I_{m}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\;|{\mathbb{P}}(g(V)>t)-{\mathbb{P}}(g(W)>t)|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\inf_{\delta>0}\Big\{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{N}{2}$}}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(\varphi_1(g))^2}{\delta^2}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\varphi_2(g)}{\delta}$}}\Big)\,\|\text{\rm Var}[V]-I_{m}\|_{\infty}\,+\,{\mathbb{P}}(|g(\xi)-t|\leq\delta)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ 4 N^2}{3 \sqrt{m}}$}}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(\varphi_1(g))^3}{\delta^3}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{3 \varphi_1(g) \, \varphi_2(g)}{\delta^2}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\varphi_3(g)}{\delta}$}}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\,\times\,\max_{i\leq m}\max\big\{\|V_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3},\|W_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3},3^{\frac{3}{4}}\big\}\Big\}\;.

Recalling that
m_{N}=\frac{m}{N^{4}}
and
\epsilon_{\rm Var}=N\,\|\text{\rm Var}[V]-I_{m}\|_{\infty}
gives the first desired bound.

Now suppose
g
is a polynomial function with degree at most
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m})
, we can apply the Carbery-Wright inequality (Theorem 8 of
8
) to obtain that, for some universal constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(|g(\xi)-t|\leq\delta)\;\leq\;C\kappa\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \delta}{\| g(\xi) \|_{L_2}}$}}\Big)^{1/\kappa}\;.

This allows us to choose

\displaystyle\delta\;=\;\max\Big\{m_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\,,\,\epsilon_{\rm Var}^{\frac{1}{2\kappa+1}}\Big\}\;=\;\max\Big\{\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{N^2}{\sqrt{m}}$}}\Big)^{\frac{\kappa}{3\kappa+1}}\,,\,\big(N\,\|\text{\rm Var}[V]-I_{m}\|_{\infty}\big)^{\frac{\kappa}{2\kappa+1}}\Big\}

to obtain

\displaystyle\;|{\mathbb{P}}(g(V)>t)-{\mathbb{P}}(g(W)>t)|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{2}$}}\,\Big((\varphi_{1}(g))^{2}\,\epsilon_{\rm Var}^{\frac{1}{2\kappa+1}}\,+\,\varphi_{2}(g)\,\epsilon_{\rm Var}^{\frac{\kappa+1}{2\kappa+1}}\Big)+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{C \kappa}{\| g(\xi) \|_{L_2}^{1/\kappa}}$}}\,\max\Big\{m_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\,,\,\epsilon_{\rm Var}^{\frac{1}{2\kappa+1}}\Big\}\;.

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ 4}{3}$}}\,\Big((\varphi_{1}(g))^{3}\,m_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\,+\,3\varphi_{1}(g)\,\varphi_{2}(g)\,m_{N}^{-\frac{\kappa+1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\,+\,\varphi_{3}(g)\,m_{N}^{-\frac{2\kappa+1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\;\times\;\max_{i\leq m}\max\big\{\|V_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3},\|W_{i}\|_{L_{4}}^{3},3^{\frac{3}{4}}\big\}\;.

Taking a supremum over
t\in\mathbb{R}
proves the second bound.
∎

E.2. Finite-sample and conditional variant of
Theorem

5

We inherit the notation and assumptions outlined in
Section

3.3
for
Theorem

5
, with one exception: Instead of imposing
7
on density smoothness, we explicitly include the following anti-concentration term in the bound:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm ac}(\delta)\;\coloneqq\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{P}}_{Z^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{nd})}\big(\,\big|f(Z^{\prime})-t\big|\leq\delta\,\,\big|\,{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}\big)\quad\text{ for }\;\;\delta>0\;.

We also denote the constant in
6
as

\displaystyle c_{4;6\nu}\;\coloneqq\;\max\nolimits_{i\leq n,j\leq d}\,\max\big\{\big\|\,\|(\Phi_{1}(X))_{ij}\|_{L_{4}|X}\,\big\|_{L_{6\nu}}\,,\,\|X_{ij}\|_{L_{4}}\big\}

Notice that
7
implies the existence of some universal constant
K>0
such that

\mathcal{P}_{\rm ac}(\delta)\leq K\delta

for all
\delta\geq 0
. In contrast, our bound below allows
\mathcal{P}_{\rm ac}(\delta)
to decay sub-linearly in
\delta
as
\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}
, which happens e.g. when
f
is a bounded-degree polynomial. See
Section

E.1
for a further discussion on anti-concentration bounds. To state the bound, we also use the notation

\displaystyle\tilde{n}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{nd}{(N_{\rm dep})^4}$}}

and

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\rm inv}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;N_{\rm dep}\,\times\,\big\|\,\|\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]-I_{m}\|_{\infty}\,\big\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}\;.

Theorem 16
.

Then under
2
and
3
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{\nu}}\;\leq\;\inf_{\delta>0}\big\{\mathcal{E}_{\rm inv}(\delta)+\mathcal{E}_{\rm uni}(\delta)+\mathcal{P}_{\rm ac}(\delta)\big\}\;,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rm inv}(\delta)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\epsilon_{\rm inv}}{2}$}}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(\varphi_{1;6\nu}(g))^2}{\delta^2}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\varphi_{2;5\nu}(g)}{\delta}$}}\Big)\;,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rm uni}(\delta)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ 4}{3 \sqrt{\tilde n}}$}}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(\varphi_{1;6\nu}(g))^3}{\delta^3}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{3 \varphi_{1;5\nu}(g) \, \varphi_{2;5\nu}(g)}{\delta^2}$}}\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\varphi_{3;4\nu}(g)}{\delta}$}}\Big)\max\big\{c_{4;6\nu}^{3}\,,\,3^{\frac{3}{4}}\big\}\;.

Suppose in addition that
f
is a polynomial function with degree at most
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists an universal constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{\nu}}\;\leq\;\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\rm inv}+\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\rm uni}+C\,\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\rm ac}\;,

where

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\rm inv}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{2}$}}\,\Big((\varphi_{1;6\nu}(g))^{2}\,\epsilon_{\rm inv}^{\frac{1}{2\kappa+1}}\,+\,\varphi_{2;5\nu}(g)\,\epsilon_{\rm inv}^{\frac{\kappa+1}{2\kappa+1}}\Big)\;,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\rm uni}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ 4}{3}$}}\,\Big((\varphi_{1;6\nu}(g))^{3}\,\tilde{n}^{-\frac{1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\,+\,3\varphi_{1;5\nu}(g)\,\varphi_{2;5\nu}(g)\,\tilde{n}^{-\frac{\kappa+1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\,+\,\varphi_{3;4\nu}(g)\,\tilde{n}^{-\frac{2\kappa+1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\Big)

\displaystyle\;\quad\;\;\times\;\max_{i\leq m}\max\big\{c^{3}_{4;6\nu},3^{\frac{3}{4}}\big\}\;,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\rm ac}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\kappa}{\| f(Z') \|_{L_2}^{1/\kappa}}$}}\,\max\Big\{\tilde{n}^{-\frac{1}{2(3\kappa+1)}}\,,\,\epsilon_{\rm inv}^{\frac{1}{2\kappa+1}}\Big\}\;.

Proof of
Theorem

16
.

This follows directly from
Theorem

14
by replacing
g
with
f
,
V
with
\Phi_{1}(X)|X
and
W
with
X
, followed by taking
\|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}\|_{L_{\nu}}
and Hölder’s inequality.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5
.

Note that under the conditions of
Theorem

5
,
\tilde{n}\rightarrow\infty
and
\epsilon_{\rm inv}\rightarrow 0
.
5
and
6
respectively provide the required upper bounds on the stability terms and
c_{4;6\nu}
, and
7
implies that
\|f(Z^{\prime})\|_{L_{2}}=\Omega(1)
. Therefore
Theorem

16
proves that
\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{\nu}}\rightarrow 0
. When additionally
1
holds, we can apply this to the bound of
Theorem

3
, which is independent of
r
, to obtain the desired uniform convergence.
∎

The bound in
Theorem

16
consist of two parts: the stability error
\mathcal{E}_{\rm uni}(\delta)
and anti-concentration bound
\mathcal{P}_{\rm ac}(\delta)
arise from the universality approximation, whereas
\mathcal{E}_{\rm inv}(\delta)
is an approximate invariance error. To interpret the bounds, we observe the following:

\bullet

Interpretation of the universality error.
Suppose that
X
has i.i.d. data with i.i.d. coordinates, and
\Phi_{1}(X)|X
also has i.i.d. data with i.i.d. coordinates (e.g. if
\Phi_{1}
performs coordinate-wise i.i.d. noise injection on the entire dataset). In this case,
N_{\rm dep}=1
. Suppose further that
f
is a degree-
\kappa
polynomial. Ignoring the approximate invariance error
\epsilon_{\rm inv}
, the bound in
Theorem

16
decays at a rate
(nd)^{-\frac{1}{6\kappa+2}}
, which gets slower for larger values of
\kappa
. The dependence on
n
may appear sub-optimal since, if e.g.
f
is a degree-two polynomial, the rate scales as
O(n^{-\frac{1}{14}})
.
45
shows that this rate is in fact nearly optimal: They construct degree-
\kappa
polynomials of random vectors such that the lower bound for the universality approximation is
\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{6\kappa}})
.
A main difference of our bound from
45
is the local dependency measure
N_{\rm dep}
: This allows for more general dependence across observations and across coordinates, provided that

\displaystyle N_{\rm dep}\;\ll\;(nd)^{1/4}\;.

As considered in
27
for high-dimensional logistic regression and
44
for general estimators, this dependence via
N_{\rm dep}
will prove to be crucial for analysing the effects of general data augmentations.

\bullet

Interpretation of the approximate invariance error.
Up to a vanishing universality approximation error,
Theorem

16
controls the approximate invariance error in Kolmogorov distance,
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
, via the approximate invariance error in the variance,
\epsilon_{\rm inv}
. This can be much easier to compute, as considered in
Appendix

G
. We also note that
\epsilon_{\rm inv}
trades off against the stability terms
\varphi_{1;6}
and
\varphi_{2;5}
, which is similar to the observation in
3
for bootstrap of general functions.

E.3. Computing local dependence measure

The measure of local dependency in
Theorem

16
is given by

\displaystyle N_{\rm dep}\;\equiv\;N_{\rm dep}(X)\;=\;\max\{N(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(X)|X})\,,\,N(\mu_{X})\}\;.

N(\mu_{X})
is determined purely by the original dataset, and imposes conditions on observation-wise and coordinate-wise dependence. In this section we focus on computing the value of

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\,N(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})})\;,

which is a deterministic upper bound to
N(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(X)|X})
and determined entirely by the distribution of
\Phi_{1}
. Throughout, given a generic vector
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{b}
, we denote
(\boldsymbol{w})_{l}
as the
l
-th coordinate of
\boldsymbol{w}
and write
(\boldsymbol{w})_{A}\coloneqq(\boldsymbol{w})_{l\in A}
for a subset
A\subseteq[b]
.

“Local” transformations.

We first consider transformations that are independent across many small subsets of coordinates.

Lemma 17

(Independent transformations in many small subsets)
.

Suppose there exists a partition
(A_{t})_{t\leq T}
of the index set
[nd]
such that for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}
, the collection of variables
\{(\Phi_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}))_{A_{t}}\}_{t\leq T}
are independent. Then
N_{*}\coloneqq\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}}N(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})})\leq\sup_{t\leq T}|A_{t}|
.

Proof.

The result follows from the definition of
N(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})})
by taking the local dependency neighbourhood of each coordinate
l
to be the
A_{t}
that contains
l
.
∎

Section

E.3
covers a range of “local” transformations used in practice. To see this, for simplicity we first assume that transformations are i.i.d. across the
n
different observations, i.e.

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(X)\;=\;(\phi_{1}(X_{1}),\ldots,\phi_{n}(X_{n}))\;,

where
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
are
n

\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random vectors and
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
are i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
transformations. Then
Section

E.3
covers the following specifications of
\phi_{1}
:

(a)

Random shuffling within small subsets of coordinates.
This is useful, for instance, in genomics data where the data vector can be divided into different groups of homogeneous coordinates
94
,
77
. Formally, this is specified by partitioning
[d]
into disjoint subsets
(\tilde{A}_{t})_{t\leq\tilde{T}}
and defining, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\leq\tilde{T}
,

\displaystyle(\phi_{1}(x))_{\tilde{A}_{t}}\coloneqq(x_{\pi_{1t}(i)})_{i\in\tilde{A}_{t}}

(40)

where each
\pi_{1t}
is an independent uniform permutation on
\tilde{A}_{t}
and
(\pi_{1t})_{t\leq\tilde{T}}
are independent.
In this case, the upper bound in
Section

E.3
is exactly
N_{*}\leq\sup_{t\leq\tilde{T}}|\tilde{A}_{t}|
;

(b)

Pixel-wise i.i.d. image augmentations, such as jittering of brightness, hue, contrast and saturation.
An image
with 3 colour channels can be modelled as an
\mathbb{R}^{3d^{\prime}}
vector
x^{\prime}
. Denote
(x^{\prime})_{l}
as the
l
-th pixel, which is a 3-dimensional vector. Then the transformations considered can then be formally specified as, for
l\in[d^{\prime}]
,

\displaystyle(\phi_{1}(x^{\prime}))_{l}\;\coloneqq\;\phi_{1l}((x^{\prime})_{l})\;,

where
(\phi_{1l})_{l\leq d^{\prime}}
are i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
transformations. In this case,
N_{*}=3
;

(c)

Coordinate-wise i.i.d. noise injection.
This can be formally specified as, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
l\leq d
,

\displaystyle(\phi_{1}(x))_{l}\;\coloneqq\;\psi_{1l}(x_{l})\;,

where
(\psi_{1l})_{l\leq d}
are i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
noise injection mechanisms, such as additive noise or multiplicative noise. In this case,
N_{*}=1
.

“Global” transformations.

It is also common to consider transformations that introduce dependence across many coordinates, and we state some examples below. For simplicity, we use
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to represent an image with 1 colour channel, as an analogous formulation holds for the case with 3 colour channels.

(a)

Random reflection of images.
Consider
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d
even. Upon an appropriate ordering of the
d
pixels, a random reflection can be formulated as the transformation
\phi
such that, for
l\leq d
,

\displaystyle(\phi_{1}(x))_{l}\;\coloneqq\;x_{\pi_{1}(l)}\;,

(41)

where
\pi_{1}:[d]\rightarrow[d]
is either the identity map or the map
l\mapsto d+1-l
with equal probabilities. In this case,
N_{*}=d
;

(b)

Random rotation of images.
Consider a stylised setup where rotation only cycles the pixels of an image
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Pixel rotation can be formulated by partitioning
[d]
into disjoint equal-sized subsets
(\tilde{A}_{t})_{t\leq\tilde{T}}
, and defining
\phi_{1}
as in (
41
), where
\pi
is now a uniform cycling operation that preserves the partition and acts in the same way on all
\tilde{A}_{t}
. For instance when
\tilde{T}=2
,
\pi_{1}
is defined by drawing uniformly a permutation
\pi^{\prime}_{1}
on
\{1,\ldots,d/2\}
and setting

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(l)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\pi^{\prime}_{1}(l)\text{ for }l\leq d/2

and

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(l)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\pi^{\prime}_{1}(d-l)\text{ for }l>d/2\;.

In this case,
N_{*}=d
;

(c)

Random permutations within large subsets of coordinates.
The formulation is identical to (
40
) except that
\sup_{t\leq\tilde{T}}|\tilde{A}_{t}|
is large in this case. For instance, random permutations of
n
data points lead to
N_{*}=n
;

(d)

Random resized cropping, random subset selection and random projection.
Another common image augmentation technique is
RandomResizedCrop
, which randomly selects a window from the image for cropping and resizes the obtained pixels to the original image size with interpolation. For simplicity we only consider a toy setup: We partition
[d]
into disjoint equal-sized subsets
(\tilde{A}_{t})_{t\leq\tilde{T}}
, draw
\tau_{1}\sim\textrm{Uniform}[\tilde{T}]
, and fill the
d
pixels by
(x)_{\tilde{A}_{\tau_{1}}}
by repeating the pixels. Formally, writing
k=d/\tilde{T}
, we define

\displaystyle(\phi_{1}(x))_{(l-1)\tilde{T}+s}\;\coloneqq\;\text{ $l$-th element of $(x)_{\tilde{A}_{\tau_{1}}}$ }

for
1\leq l\leq k
and
1\leq s\leq\tilde{T}
. Note that this formulation also covers random disjoint subset selection and random projections onto disjoint subsets of data. In this case,
N_{*}=d
;

(e)

Mixup augmentation.
Mixup is an augmentation technique that has seen popularity across a large range of data domains, including images, text, audio and video data
[
12
,
63
,
92
,
76
,
49
]
. The exact implementation varies across applications, but the shared core idea is to synthesise artificial data points that interpolate between the original data points. We again consider a simplified formulation, which mirrors the random convex combination adopted in the original mixup paper
[
93
]
: For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we draw
\omega_{i}\sim\textrm{Uniform}[0,1]
and
(\tau_{i1},\tau_{i2})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d. uniformly drawn two-tuples of distinct numbers from
[n]
. A “bootstrapped” version of mixup can then be described as

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(X)\;=\;(\tilde{\phi}_{1}(X),\ldots,\tilde{\phi}_{n}(X))\;,\quad\text{ where }\qquad\tilde{\phi}_{i}(X)\;=\;\omega_{i}X_{\tau_{i1}}+(1-\omega_{i})X_{\tau_{i2}}\;.

In this case,
N_{*}=d
, since the mixup weight
\omega_{i}
and the data indices
(\tau_{i1},\tau_{i2})
are all chosen independently across the
n
observations but shared across all coordinates.

For the transformations above,
N_{*}
is large, which hurts the bound in
Theorem

16
. There are two remedies:

(a)

For
N_{*}=d
,
N_{\rm dep}^{2}/\sqrt{nd}=O(d^{3/2}n^{-1/2})
. This introduces a constraint on the dimensionality
d=o(n^{1/3})
;

(b)

For
N_{*}=n
e.g. in (c) above,
N_{\rm dep}^{2}/\sqrt{nd}=O(n^{3/2}d^{-1/2})
, which does not converge when
d
is fixed. Nevertheless, we will only consider this in the set up of a composite transformation

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}\;=\;\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\;,

where
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1}
are independent random transformations:
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is an exactly exchangeable, inter-observation transformation e.g. permutations and cycling of
n
exchangeable data points, whereas

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\;=\;(\tilde{\phi}_{i})_{i\leq n}

consists of
n
i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
random transformations each acting on a data point. In this case, we will first apply
Section

3.4
in
Section

4
, which reduces the analysis of
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
to the sole effect of
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
. The local dependency measure corresponding to
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
can then be bounded by a quantity completely determined by
\tilde{\phi}_{1}
, and the corresponding computation of
N_{*}
is now independent of
n
.

Composing two approximately invariant transformations when one of them is linear.
Denote
\Phi_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
as above. When both
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
satisfy only approximate invariance,
Section

3.4
does not apply, and the local dependency measure
N(\mu_{\Phi_{1}(X)|X})
can be troublesome to compute. Nevertheless, when
\mathcal{T}_{1}
takes value in the set of linear transformations, it suffices to consider the local dependency measures

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}}N(\mu_{\text{\rm Var}[\mathcal{T}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})]^{-1/2}\mathcal{T}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})})\qquad\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\qquad\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}}N(\mu_{\text{\rm Var}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})]^{-1/2}\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})})\;,

which are quantities that separately concern the dependencies introduced by
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
. This is achieved by a two-step application of our general universality result (
Theorem

14
with
Remark

7
):

\bullet

We first condition on
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and apply universality to the function
f(\mathcal{T}_{1}({\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}))
, whose stability properties are the same as
f
by the linearity of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
. This allows us to obtain, conditionally on
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
X
, the distributional approximation

\displaystyle f(\mathcal{T}_{1}(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)))\;\overset{d}{\approx}

\displaystyle\;f\big(\mathcal{T}_{1}\big(\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]+\sqrt{\text{\rm Var}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]}\,\xi\big)\big)

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;f\big(\underbrace{\mathcal{T}_{1}\big(\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\big)\,+\,\mathcal{T}_{1}\big(\sqrt{\text{\rm Var}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]}\,\xi\big)}_{\eqqcolon(\star\star)}\big)\;,

where
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{nd})
is independent of all other variables and the second line above follows by linearity of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
. This step incurs an error that can be controlled purely by the local dependency measure
\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}}N(\mu_{\text{\rm Var}[\mathcal{T}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})]^{-1/2}\mathcal{T}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})})
;

\bullet

Next, we apply universality conditionally on
X
to approximate
(\star\star)
by a Gaussian, whose conditional mean and variance can be computed by the linearity of
\mathcal{T}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\star\star)|X]\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\big(\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\big)\big]

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)\,|\,X]\;=\;\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]\;,

\displaystyle\text{\rm Var}[(\star\star)|X]\;=

\displaystyle\;\text{\rm Var}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\big(\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\big)+\mathcal{T}_{1}\big(\sqrt{\text{\rm Var}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]}\,\xi\big)\,\big|\,X\big]

\displaystyle\;\overset{(a)}{=}

\displaystyle\;\text{\rm Var}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\big(\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\big)\,\big|\,X\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\text{\rm Var}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\top}\,\big|\,X\big]

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\,\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]^{\top}\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\top}\big]

\displaystyle\;-\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\,\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]^{\top}

\displaystyle\;+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\text{\rm Var}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\top}\,\big|\,X\big]

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)\Phi_{1}(X)^{\top}|X]\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\top}\big]

\displaystyle\;-\;\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]\,\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)|X]^{\top}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\text{\rm Var}[\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X)\,|\,X]\;=\;\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)\,|\,X]\;.

In
(a)
above, we have used the total law of variance. In other words, the second application of universality gives the correct Gaussian surrogate for
\Phi_{1}(X)|X
. Moreover, it incurs an error controllable purely by the local dependency measure
\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}}N(\mu_{\text{\rm Var}[\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})]^{-1/2}\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})})
.

In summary, for composite transformations
\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
where
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is linear, it suffices to consider the local dependency measures related to
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
individually, and all computations in this section still apply. A formal statement of the universality result requires stating the stability interpolation paths
\mathcal{C}
for the two applications of universality, which is notationally cumbersome and hence omitted here.

E.4. Verification of stability conditions

We verify stability conditions for two examples in this section: A polynomial estimator, which covers most estimators considered in this work, and the squared loss statistic used in conformal prediction, which demonstrates the lack of stability for vanilla conformal prediction residual.

Polynomial estimators.
We consider estimators in the class of symmetric and polynomial
\mathbb{R}^{nd}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
functions. This can be extended to cover estimators that can be approximated by an
\kappa
-order Taylor expansion and that are symmetric under permutations of data entries. For the next result only, we view
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}
as an
nd
-dimensional vector and use the single index
(\boldsymbol{x})_{i}
to denote the
i
-th coordinate of
\boldsymbol{x}
for
1\leq i\leq nd
.

Lemma 18
.

Fix
\nu\geq 1
. Suppose
f=p_{\kappa}
, where
p_{\kappa}:\mathbb{R}^{nd}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a degree-
\kappa
polynomial that takes the form

\displaystyle p_{\kappa}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{nd})\;\coloneqq\;\alpha_{0}+\msum_{l=1}^{\kappa}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\alpha_l}{(nd)^{l/2}}$}}\msum_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\leq nd}\,(x_{i_{1}}\times\cdots\times\,x_{i_{l}})\Big)

for
\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha_{\rm max}\coloneqq\max_{1\leq l\leq\kappa}|\alpha_{l}|
. Then for
r=1,2,3
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{r;\nu}\;\leq\;\alpha_{\rm max}\,\msum_{l=1}^{\kappa}\Big(2^{l}\,\max\Big\{

\displaystyle\;\Big\|\,\big\|\tilde{p}_{l-r}\big(\Phi_{1}(X)\big)\big\|_{L_{4}|X}\,\Big\|_{L_{\nu}}\;,\;\big\|\,\tilde{p}_{l-r}(X^{\prime})\,\big\|_{L_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\;\Big\|\,\big\|\tilde{p}_{l-r}\big(Z^{\Phi}(X)\big)\big\|_{L_{4}|X}\,\Big\|_{L_{\nu}}\;,\;\big\|\,\tilde{p}_{l-r}(Z^{\prime})\,\big\|_{L_{\nu}}\Big\}\Big)\;,

where, for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have denoted

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{s}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{nd})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{nd}}\msum_{i=1}^{nd}x_{i}\big)^{s}&\text{ if }s\geq 0\;,\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof of
Section

E.4
.

This follows from applying the definition (
13
) and noting that, for
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{i})_{i\leq nd}
and
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{i})_{i\leq nd}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{s}(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})\;=

\displaystyle\;\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{nd}}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{nd}(x_{i}+y_{i})\Big)^{s}

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{s-1}\,\Big(\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{nd}}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{nd}x_{i}\Big)^{s}+\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{nd}}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{nd}y_{i}\Big)^{s}\Big)\;.

(42)

∎

The
L_{\nu}
norm of
\tilde{p}_{s}
can typically be shown to be
O\big(N_{\rm dep}^{s/2}\big)
under independence, martingale dependence or mixing conditions; see e.g.
62
,
21
,
85
,
25
. The
2^{m}
-dependence can be removed by controlling the norm of
\tilde{p}_{s}
directly on the interpolation path rather than applying the crude inequality in (
42
); this is treated in
45
, which provides a stability control in the case
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are independent and provided that
\kappa=o(\log(n))
. We also refer readers to
45
for two detailed discussions:

\bullet

45
includes a generic treatment for asymmetric polynomials and approximately polynomial estimators: There, they also show that those polynomials covers a large class of estimators such as high-dimensional U-statistics and V-statistics, as well as other estimators that can be approximated by a delta method.

\bullet

While the stability terms
\varphi_{r;\nu}
can additionally introduce factors of the form
N_{\rm dep}^{s/2}
,
45
also shows that depending on the precise dependence structure, these additional factors may cancel out with
\|f(Z^{\prime})\|_{L_{2}}
, introduced by the anti-concentration term in (
16
). The precise condition on how much
N_{\rm dep}
is allowed to grow with
n
and
d
then depends on individual estimators.

Split conformal prediction in the high-dimensional regime.
We consider split conformal prediction with the squared loss error: For
X=(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\leq n}
consisting of random variables
X_{i}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
Y_{i}\coloneqq g_{\rm oracle}(X_{i})
, we consider

\displaystyle f(X)\;\coloneqq\;|Y_{1}-g_{\rm trained}(X_{1})|^{2}\;=\;|g_{\rm oracle}(X_{1})-g_{\rm trained}(X_{1})|^{2}\;,

where
g_{\rm trained}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is trained on a separate dataset and
g_{\rm oracle}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the oracle output-generating function. This statistic appears to be different from those considered in existing universality literature, because the partial derivatives of
f
with respect to
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i>1}
are all zero, but the partial derivative with respect to
(X_{1},Y_{1})
is non-negligible. To address this case, we note that for the purpose of combining data augmentation with conformal prediction, a typical transformation takes the form

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}\;=\;\pi_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\;,

where
\pi_{1}
is a random cycling of the data index set
[n]
, whereas the approximately invariant transformation reads

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{1}((X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n}))\;=

\displaystyle\;((\phi_{1}(X_{1}),Y_{1}),\ldots,(\phi_{n}(X_{n}),Y_{n}))\;,

(43)

where
\phi_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
are random transformations that act only on individual inputs, and the outputs are left unchanged. Using
Section

3.4
to condition on the permutation
\pi_{1}
, which satisfies exact invariance, the estimators to compare via universality take the form

\displaystyle f(\pi_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X))\;=

\displaystyle\;\big|g_{\rm oracle}(X_{\pi_{1}(1)})-g_{\rm trained}(\phi_{\pi_{1}(1)}(X_{\pi_{1}(1)}))\big|^{2}\;,

\displaystyle f(\pi_{1}(X))\;=

\displaystyle\;\big|g_{\rm oracle}(X_{\pi_{1}(1)})-g_{\rm trained}(X_{\pi_{1}(1)})\big|^{2}\;.

Therefore, conditioning on
\pi_{1}
, we only need to apply universality to control

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(\tilde{f}(X_{\pi_{1}(1)},\phi_{\pi_{1}(1)}(X_{\pi_{1}(1)}))\leq t\,|\,X_{\pi_{1}(1)},\pi_{1})\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(\tilde{f}(X_{\pi_{1}(1)},X_{\pi_{1}(1)})\leq t\,|\,\pi_{1})\big|\;,

where we have defined the function
\tilde{f}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(x,x^{\prime})\;\coloneqq\;|g_{\rm oracle}(x)-g_{\rm trained}(x^{\prime})|^{2}\;.

In the high-dimensional regime where
d\rightarrow\infty
and where the coordiantes of the data exhibits sufficient independence, we can then apply
Theorem

16
with
n=1
, and exploit local dependence of data coordiantes and of transformations. The next lemma controls
\tilde{\varphi}_{r;\nu}
, the corresponding stability measure of
\tilde{f}
, under a similar setup as
Section

E.4
.

Lemma 19
.

Assume that
g_{\rm trained}
and
g_{\rm oracle}
both take the form of
p_{m}
in
Section

E.4
with
n
replaced by
1
, and that the corresponding
\alpha_{l}
’s are uniformly bounded in norm by
\alpha_{\rm max}
. Also assume the form of
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
in (
43
), and that
X_{i}
’s are i.i.d. generated. Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that, for
r=1,2,3
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{r;\nu}\;\leq\;C\,\alpha_{\rm max}\,\msum_{l=1}^{m}\Big(2^{l}\,\max\Big\{

\displaystyle\;\Big\|\,\big\|\hat{p}_{2l-r}\big(\phi_{1}(X_{1})\big)\big\|_{L_{4}|X}\,\Big\|_{L_{\nu}|{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}}\;,\;\big\|\,\hat{p}_{2l-r}(X_{1})\,\big\|_{L_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\;\Big\|\,\big\|\hat{p}_{2l-r}\big(Z^{\phi}_{1}(X_{1})\big)\big\|_{L_{4}|X}\,\Big\|_{L_{\nu}|{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}}\;,\;\big\|\,\hat{p}_{2l-r}(Z_{1})\,\big\|_{L_{\nu}}\Big\}\Big)\;,

where, for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have denoted for
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{s}(x_{1})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\msum_{l=1}^{d}x_{1l}\big)^{s}&\text{ if }s\geq 0\;,\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

and
Z^{\phi}_{1}(X_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{E}[\phi_{1}(X_{1})|X_{1}],\text{\rm Var}[\phi_{1}(X_{1})|X_{1}])
,
Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}],\text{\rm Var}[X_{1}|{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}])
.

Proof of
Section

E.4
.

Applying the chain rule and the same argument as
Section

E.4
gives the desired bound.
∎

We have assumed that
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
does not change the output and that
X_{i}
’s are i.i.d. for simplicity of presentation, but the same argument extends to the cases where both conditions are violated. The more crucial condition is that, due to the form of the residual error, universality (
Theorem

16
) can only be applied to control
\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)
when:

\bullet

we operate in a high-dimensional regime, where
d\rightarrow\infty
, and

\bullet

both the data and the transformations satisfy a coordinate-wise local dependence condition. This notably excludes the “global” transformations in
Section

E.3
.

As a consequence, this restricts the class of transformations that can be used for conformal prediction to enjoy Gaussian universality guarantees.

Appendix F
Proof of
Section

3.4

In the case
\Phi_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
, by the invariance of
X
under
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, we can write

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\;=

\displaystyle\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(\mathcal{T}_{1}(X))\leq t)\big|

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\mathcal{T}}(X)\leq t)\big|

\displaystyle\;\overset{(a)}{=}

\displaystyle\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\,{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X,f_{\mathcal{T}})\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\mathcal{T}}(X)\leq t\,|\,f_{\mathcal{T}})\,\big|X\big]\big|

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X,f_{\mathcal{T}})\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\mathcal{T}}(X)\leq t\,|\,f_{\mathcal{T}})\big|\,\Big|\,X\Big]\;.

In
(a)
, we have separated out the expectation over
f_{\mathcal{T}}
and coupled
f_{\mathcal{T}}
in the two additive terms. In the case
\Phi_{1}=\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}
, we use conditional invariance to note that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\;=

\displaystyle\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{E}[{\mathbb{P}}(f(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X,\mathcal{T}_{1})\,|\,X]\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|

\displaystyle\;\overset{(b)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,\mathcal{T}_{1}(X))\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|\,\Big|\,X\Big]\;.

In
(b)
, we have used Jensen’s inequality to move
\mathbb{E}[{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}|X]
outside. Taking
\|{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}\|_{L_{\nu}}
on both sides and recalling that
\nu\geq 1
, we can apply Jensen’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\rm inv}(X)\|_{L_{\nu}}\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\Big\|\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,\mathcal{T}_{1}(X))\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|\Big\|_{L_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\Big\|\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(f(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}(X))\leq t\,|\,X)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}(f(X)\leq t)\big|\Big\|_{L_{\nu}}\;.

In the last line, we have replaced
\mathcal{T}_{1}(X)
by
X
using invariance.
∎

Appendix G
Invariance conditions for the examples in
Section

4

In this section, we characterise and discuss the approximate invariance conditions for the examples in
Section

4
.

G.1. Invariance conditions for observation-wise augmentations and variants of bootstrap in
Section

4.1

For observation-wise augmentations, i.e. DAB with
\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}
, we note that the discussion in
Section

3.4
applies to both setups in
Section

4.1
. Specifically:

\bullet

For orthogonal transformations on mean-zero and isotropic random vectors, the conditional mean and variance in (
18
) can be computed as

\displaystyle\mu_{\phi}(X)\;=

\displaystyle\;0

and

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\phi}(X)\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\;\xrightarrow{\rm a.s.}\;I_{d}\;,

which match
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]=0
and
\text{\rm Var}[X_{1}]=I_{d}
;

\bullet

For the parallel sampling setup, (
18
) suggests that we may still have a valid CI provided that

\displaystyle\;\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[h\big(\phi_{11}(X^{(t)}_{1})\big)\,\Big|\,X^{(t)}_{1}\Big]\,,\,\text{\rm Var}\Big[h\big(\phi_{11}(X^{(t)}_{1})\big)\,\Big|\,X^{(t)}_{1}\Big]\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\;\approx\;\big(\mathbb{E}\big[h(X^{(t)}_{1})\big]\,,\,\text{\rm Var}\big[h(X^{(t)}_{1})\big]\big)\,.

Note that this condition can be unverifiable in general, both due to the intractable conditioning and due to the unknown mean and variance of
h(X^{(t)}_{1})
.

In contrast to the above, the bootstrap resampling step in DAB with
\Phi^{\rm bootstrap}_{b}\circ\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}
allows for marginal moment matching. To see this, let
\phi_{i}
’s be i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
transformations as in
Section

4.1
and
\tau_{bi}\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\textrm{Uniform}([n])
represent the bootstrap resampling of data indices. For the theoretical discussion here, we consider statistics
f
that satisfy the stability conditions required in
Theorem

5
, and for experiments, we focus on empirical averages for simplicity. We WLOG let
X_{i}
’s be i.i.d. mean-zero random vectors; this is automatically satisfied for the empirical average in
Example

1
where
X=(\tilde{X}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}_{1}])_{i\leq n}
, and the general non-zero-mean case can be handled by an additional stability requirement at the expense of more notation (see
Section

3.3
). In this case,
\Phi_{b}=\Phi^{\rm bootstrap}_{b}\circ\Phi^{\rm new}_{b}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Phi_{b}(X)\;=

\displaystyle\;\big(\phi_{\tau_{b1}}(X_{\tau_{b1}})-\bar{X}_{\phi}\,,\,\ldots\,,\,\phi_{\tau_{bn}}(X_{\tau_{bn}})-\bar{X}_{\phi}\big)\;,\quad\bar{X}_{\phi}\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i\leq n}\phi_{i}(X_{i})\;.

When
Theorem

5
holds, to obtain valid coverage, it suffices to compute the conditional mean and variance:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]\;=\;\mathbb{E}\big[\,\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{1}(X)\,|\,X,(\phi_{i})_{i\leq n}]\,\big|\,X\big]\;\overset{(a)}{=}\;0\;=\;\mathbb{E}[X]\;,

where
(a)
follows from the empirical centering in bootstrap.
\text{\rm Var}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]
is an
\mathbb{R}^{nd\times nd}
block diagonal matrix, consisting of identical
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
blocks of entries given by

\displaystyle\;\text{\rm Var}\big[\phi_{\tau_{1}}(X_{\tau_{1}})-\bar{X}_{\phi}\,\big|\,X\big]

\displaystyle\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{n-1}{n^2}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X_{i})\phi_{i}(X_{i})^{\top}|X_{i}]-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n^2}$}}\msum_{i\neq j}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X_{i})|X_{i}]\;\mathbb{E}[\phi_{j}(X_{j})|X_{j}]^{\top}\;.

In particular, the variance difference term in
Theorem

5
can be controlled as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\Big\|\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{n-1}{n^2}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X_{i})\phi_{i}(X_{i})^{\top}|X_{i}]-\text{\rm Var}[X_{1}]\Big\|_{\infty}

(44)

\displaystyle\;+\Big\|\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n^2}$}}\msum_{i\neq j}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X_{i})|X_{i}]\;\mathbb{E}[\phi_{j}(X_{j})|X_{j}]^{\top}\Big\|_{\infty}\;.

(45)

Under an additional condition on
\phi_{1}
that
\mathbb{E}[\phi_{1}(X_{1})]=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]=0
and a moment boundedness condition, by Nemirovski’s moment inequality (see e.g. Lemma 14.24 of
6
) we have

\displaystyle\|\eqref{eq:bootstrap:augment:var:diff}\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}\;=

\displaystyle\;O\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sqrt{\log d} \, \| \Var[ \phi_1(X_1) ] - \Var[X_1] \|_\infty}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\Big)\;,

(46)

and under an additional uniform sub-Gaussian tail condition on the coordinates of
\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X_{i})\phi_{i}(X_{i})^{\top}|X_{i}]
, by the Hanson-Wright inequality (see e.g.
85
),

\displaystyle\|\eqref{eq:bootstrap:augment:cov}\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}\;=

\displaystyle\;O\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\log d}{n}$}}\Big)\;.

As such, by the conditions of
Theorem

5
, we have valid coverage for any choice of
\phi_{i}
’s such that

\displaystyle N_{\rm dep}\;=

\displaystyle\;o\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{n}{\log d}$}}\Big)

and

\displaystyle\|\text{\rm Var}[\phi_{1}(X_{1})]-\text{\rm Var}[X_{1}]\|_{\infty}\;=

\displaystyle\;o\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sqrt{n}}{N_{\rm dep}\, \sqrt{\log d} }$}}\Big)\;.

Importantly, bootstrap resampling allows the conditional moment matching in
Theorem

5
to be improved to
marginal
moment matching, and a sufficient condition for
\Delta_{\rm Var}(X)
to be small is the marginal distributional invariance

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(X_{1})\;\overset{d}{=}\;X_{1}\;.

G.2. Invariance conditions for wild bootstrap with additional transformations in
Section

4.2

Notice that wild bootstrap in
Section

4.2
is applied only to degree-two U-statistics. By
Section

E.4
and its subsequent discussion, we expect the universality result of
Theorem

5
to apply under additional coordinate dependence conditions on
X_{1}
and
\phi(X_{1})
as well as a condition that the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is well-approximated by a subspace of some ambient Euclidean space. In this section, we show that for the DAB variant of wild bootstrap introduced in
Section

4.2
, when
Theorem

5
holds, the approximate invariance condition reduces to a conditional moment matching condition in the feature map space
\varphi
.

Let
(\phi_{ri})_{r\in\{1,2\},i\leq n}
be i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
transformations that are identically distributed as
\phi
, as defined in
Section

4.2
. Suppose we have the additional knowledge that
P
exhibits approximate invariance with respect to
\phi
in the sense that the probability measure
P_{\phi}
of
\phi(X_{1})
satisfies

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}(P,P_{\phi})\leq\epsilon

for some probability metric
\boldsymbol{d}
and
\epsilon>0
to be specified later. The two-sample test problem is the same as testing

\displaystyle H^{\phi}_{0}:\;P=Q\,,\,\boldsymbol{d}(P,P_{\phi})\leq\epsilon\qquad\text{ v.s. }\qquad H^{\phi}_{1}:\;P\neq Q\,,\,\boldsymbol{d}(P,P_{\phi})\leq\epsilon\;.

Recall that our dataset is
\{Y_{i},Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, where
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are samples from
P
and
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are samples from
Q
. Also recall that
\varphi
are the feature maps such that the kernel function
\kappa(y,y^{\prime})=\langle\varphi(y),\varphi(y^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. We can express DAB in
Section

4.2
as the transformation
\Phi_{1}
that acts on a dataset
(y_{i},z_{i})_{i\leq n}
as

\displaystyle\;\Phi_{1}\big(\varphi(y_{1})-\varphi(z_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(y_{n})-\varphi(z_{n})\big)

\displaystyle\;=\;\big(\epsilon_{1}\,\times\,(\varphi(\phi_{11}(y_{1}))-\varphi(\phi_{21}(z_{1})))\,,\,\ldots\,,\,\epsilon_{n}\,\times\,(\varphi(\phi_{1n}(y_{n}))-\varphi(\phi_{2n}(z_{n})))\big)\;,

where
\epsilon_{i}
’s are i.i.d. Rademacher random variables as in (
9
). While
\varphi
is intractable and therefore
\Phi_{1}
is not directly computable, its corresponding statistic
f(\Phi_{1}(X))

is
computable, giving the expression

\displaystyle f(\Phi_{1}(X))\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n(n-1)}$}}\msum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{j}\,u\big(\,\phi_{1i}(Y_{i}),\phi_{1j}(Y_{j}),\phi_{2i}(Z_{i}),\phi_{2j}(Z_{j})\big)\;,

which recovers the expression (
23
) in the main text.

To formulate the approximate invariance condition by applying
Theorem

5
, we take for simplicity that
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{q}
with some
q\in\mathbb{N}
, so that the feature maps
\varphi
have Euclidean outputs. We first observe that since
\epsilon_{i}
’s are zero-mean and independent,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{1}(X)|X]\;=\;0\;\overset{H^{\phi}_{0}}{=}\;\mathbb{E}[X]\;,

which implies both conditional and marginal mean matching. For variance, note that

\displaystyle\;\text{\rm Var}\big[\epsilon_{1}\times(\varphi(\phi_{11}(Y_{1}))-\varphi(\phi_{21}(Z_{1})))\,\big|\,(Y_{i},Z_{i})_{i\leq n}\big]

\displaystyle\;=\;\text{\rm Var}\big[(\varphi(\phi_{11}(Y_{1}))-\varphi(\phi_{21}(Z_{1})))\,\big|\,Y_{1},Z_{1}\big]\;,

so the approximate invariance condition is that the conditional variance matching error

\displaystyle\big\|\big\|\text{\rm Var}\big[(\varphi(\phi_{11}(Y_{1}))-\varphi(\phi_{21}(Z_{1})))\,\big|\,Y_{1},Z_{1}\big]-\text{\rm Var}\big[(\varphi(\phi_{11}(Y_{1}))-\varphi(\phi_{21}(Z_{1})))\big]\big\|_{\infty}\big\|_{L_{3\nu/2}}

is small. Note that the above reasoning gives coverage validity under
H^{\phi}_{0}
but not necessarily so under
H^{\phi}_{1}
. This gives a valid test statistic for testing
H^{\phi}_{0}
against
H^{\phi}_{1}
.

A notable limitation is that the approximate invariance condition above can be difficult to verify, due to (i) the conditioning and (ii) the fact that the moments are in terms of the abstract feature maps
\varphi
, which can be intractable to compute. One may hope to relax (i) to marginal moment matching by incorporating bootstrap and a similar argument as
Section

G.1
. However, it is known
[
2
]
that for a degree-two degenerate U-statistics, a consistent bootstrap typically requires performing a bootstrap on its second-order Hoeffding decomposition with centering. In our notation, this corresponds to bootstrap with empirical centering by quantities involving
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}\varphi(\phi_{1i}(Y_{i}))
and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}\varphi(\phi_{2i}(Z_{i}))
, which can again be computationally intractable.

G.3. Invariance conditions for conformal prediction with data augmentation in
Section

4.3

Under
\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{b}
defined in
Section

4.3
, the statistics used in forming the DAB CIs can be expressed as

\displaystyle f\big(\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{bj}(X)\big)\;=\;h\Big(\phi^{\rm out}_{bj}(Y_{b+1})\,,\,g\Big(\phi^{\rm in}_{bj}(V_{b+1})\Big)\Big)\;,

(47)

where we have taken
(\phi^{\rm in}_{bj},\phi^{\rm out}_{bj})
as i.i.d. copy of
(\phi^{\rm in},\phi^{\rm out})
. Unlike the previous examples, we can no longer use universality for coverage guarantees:
f(X)
is completely determined by
X_{1}
, and therefore violates the stability condition required for universality in
Theorem

5
except for specific high-dimensional cases (see
Section

E.4
for details). Moreover, one should not attempt to fix this by e.g. bootstrap resampling, since to test whether
X_{1}
behaves differently from the calibration set, the conformal prediction score
f(X)
ought to be sensitive to changes in
X_{1}
. On the other hand, the remaining results in
Section

3
still apply for characterising the loss in coverage as a result of approximate invariance. In particular for
k=1
, since
(f(\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{b}(X)))_{b\leq B}
are i.i.d. and
f(X),f(\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{1}(X)),\ldots,f(\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{n-1}(X))
are independent, we may apply a variant of
Theorem

3
(
Theorem

8
in the appendix) that relaxes the conditioning on
X
and quantifies the approximate invariance error via the
marginal
c.d.f. error

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm CP}_{\rm inv}\;\coloneqq\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}\big(h(\phi^{\rm out}(Y_{1}),\,g(\phi^{\rm in}(V_{1})))\leq t\big)\,-\,{\mathbb{P}}\big(h(Y_{1},\,g(V_{1}))\leq t\big)\big|\;.

Corollary 20
.

Assume the use of smoothed uniform tie-breaking, and write
R_{\rm DAB}(X)
as the rank variable corresponding to
(\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{bj})_{b\leq n-1,j\leq k}
defined in
Section

G.3
. Let
r\in[0,1]
,
\nu\geq 1
and
n\geq 3
. Then for
k=1
,

\displaystyle\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R_{\rm DAB}(X)}{n}$}}<r\Big)\,-\,r\Big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;16\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{3}{8}$}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{\nu+2}}\,\big\|\Delta^{\rm CP}_{\rm inv}\big\|_{L_{\nu}}^{\frac{2\nu}{\nu+2}}\,+\,2\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{3}{8}$}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\nu+2}}\,\big\|\Delta^{\rm CP}_{\rm inv}\big\|_{L_{\nu}}^{\frac{\nu}{\nu+2}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n-2}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2}{\sqrt{n-1}}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sqrt{3 \log(n-1)}}{\sqrt{n-2}}$}}\Big)\;.

Proof of
Section

G.3
.

In the case
k=1
, we have that
\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{b1}(X)
are independent across
1\leq b\leq n-1
. Therefore taking
{\mathbb{X}_{\rm obs}}=\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}_{\rm trivial}
in
Theorem

8
gives the desired bound.
∎

As with
Theorem

3
,
Section

G.3
controls the coverage loss via the approximate invariance error
\Delta^{\rm CP}_{\rm inv}
. Since the error measures the difference between marginal c.d.f.s,

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm CP}_{\rm inv}\;=

\displaystyle\;0

if and only if

\displaystyle h(\phi^{\rm out}_{11}(Y_{1}),\,g(\phi^{\rm in}_{11}(V_{1})))\;\text{ equals }

\displaystyle\;h(Y_{1},g(V_{1}))\;\text{ in distribution}\;.

Observe that, when restricted to group transformations, DAB with (
47
) is a special case of SymmPI
[
23
]
, which generalises conformal prediction to incorporate invariant group transformations. Our DAB variant of conformal prediction can therefore be interpreted as an extension of SymmPI in two ways:

(a)

When exact invariance holds in the sense that
\Delta^{\rm CP}_{\rm inv}=0
, DAB extends SymmPI by allowing for non-group transformations;

(b)

In view of
Section

G.3
, DAB also extends SymmPI to allow for only approximately invariant transformations, as long as
\Delta^{\rm CP}_{\rm inv}
is small.

Orthogonal transformations for mean-zero and isotropic random vectors.
The setup in
Figure

2
and
Figures

9
,
10
and
11
is as described in
Section

4.1
with
d=2
and three different data distributions:

\bullet

2d Gaussian.

X_{i}\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{2})
;

\bullet

2d Rademacher.

X_{i}
are i.i.d. 2d random vectors and the two coordinates
X_{11}
and
X_{12}
are i.i.d. Rademacher random variables;

\bullet

2d centred Gamma vectors.

X_{i}
are i.i.d. 2d random vectors and the two coordinates
X_{11}
and
X_{12}
are i.i.d. distributed as

\displaystyle W-\mathbb{E}[W]\;,\qquad\text{ where }\qquad W\sim\Gamma(1,1)\;.

Statistics on parallel sampling from invariant distributions in AI-for-science.
In
Figures

3
and
12
, we draw MCMC samples from a trained FermiNet
[
68
]
that models the distribution of the
3
electrons in a ground-state Lithium atom. The sampling algorithm is chosen as the default Metropolis-Hastings algorithm in FermiNet. For augmentations, we take the group of simultaneous rotations on all three electron positions,

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}\;=\;\{(g,g,g)\,|\,g\in\mathbb{S}\mathbb{O}_{2}\}\;,

where
\mathbb{S}\mathbb{O}_{2}
denotes the group of simultaneous rotations in the
x
-
y
plane about the
z
-axis. The test function of interest is the
x
-component of the electron dipole moment of the system: Given a three electron configuration
x=(x_{a}^{\top},x_{b}^{\top},x_{c}^{\top})
with
x_{a},x_{b},x_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

\displaystyle h(x)\;=\;(x_{a})_{1}+(x_{b})_{1}+(x_{c})_{1}\;.

For a true sample of the three-electron configuration
X^{*}=((X^{*}_{a})^{\top},(X^{*}_{b})^{\top},(X^{*}_{c})^{\top})^{\top}
from the ground-state Lithium atom, it is known that
\mathbb{E}[(X^{*}_{a})_{1}+(X^{*}_{b})_{1}+(X^{*}_{c})_{1}]=0
and that the joint three-electron distribution is invariant under
\mathbb{G}
, although neither is guaranteed for the
t
-th step MCMC distribution
X^{(t)}
.

H.3. Experiments for conformal prediction with data augmentation in
Section

4.3

For both split conformal prediction (CP) and its DAB variant, we always set the number of conformal prediction transformations
B=n-1
.

(a) Linear regression on simulated data.

Figure

7
(i) and
Figures

24
,
25
and
26
performs conformal prediction (CP) and its DAB variants under the linear model with additive Gaussian noise and with one of the three 2d data distributions defined in
Section

H.1
. The augmentations considered are the same as the wild bootstrap setup in (a) of
Section

H.3
. The behaviours of CP and DAB are similar across the three data distributions: DAB has better coverage at small
n
but is more conservative than CP at large
n
. The coverage improvement in
Figure

7
(i) and
Figure

24
is visible with
k=50
; for comparison, we also report results with
k=1
in
Figure

25
and varying
k
in
Figure

26
to show that the improvement mainly comes from using a larger
k
to incorporate more augmentations.

Figure 27
:
Visualisation of selected molecules from the QM-symm database
[
60
]
with C4h point group symmetry.

(b) Neural networks fitted on molecular data.
We consider the CP score function proposed by
42
applied to the GMP+SNN neural network (Gaussian multi-pole featurisation
58
followed by the SingleNN architecture
[
61
]
) and to the data set of C4h-symmetric molecules in the QM-sym database
[
60
]
(visualised in
Figure

27
). Specifically:

\bullet

We first split the data set into the training set
X^{\rm train}
and holdout set
X^{\rm holdout}
;

\bullet

We train the GMP+SNN neural network on
X^{\rm train}
. Denote
\mathcal{X}
as the molecular input space. The trained network gives us a fitted predictor function
\hat{E}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for the energy, as well as a fitted latent feature map
\hat{g}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{64}
that represents the latent embedding in the last hidden layer of the network (see Figure 1 of
42
);

\bullet

We uniformly sample
n-1
molecule-energy pairs from
X^{\rm holdout}
as calibration data, denoted as
(X_{2},E_{2}),\ldots,(X_{n},E_{n})
, as well as an additional data point
(X_{1},E_{1})
as the test data point. The goal is to form a confidence interval for
E_{1}
;

\bullet

Let
n_{\rm train}=|X^{\rm train}|
. In the CP score function proposed by
42
, for any given
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we write
X^{\rm train}_{1}(x),\ldots,X^{\rm train}_{n_{\rm train}}(x)
as an enumeration of
X^{\rm train}
ordered according to their Euclidean distance in the neural network feature space to
x
, i.e. according to
d(x^{\prime})=\|\hat{g}(x^{\prime})-\hat{g}(x)\|
. This yields the extended dataset

\displaystyle X\;=\;\big(X_{i},E_{i},X^{\rm train}_{1}(X_{i}),\ldots,X^{\rm train}_{n_{\rm train}}(X_{i})\big)_{1\leq i\leq n}\;.

The conformal score function then gives

\displaystyle f(X)\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{| E_1 - \hat E( X_1 )|}{\frac{1}{M} \sum_{m=1}^M \| \hat g(X_1) - \hat g( X^{\rm train}_m(X_1) ) \| }$}}\;,

i.e. the residual error in energy prediction is reweighted by the average Euclidean distance from
X_{1}
to its
M
closest neighbours. With
\Phi^{\rm CP}_{b}
defined in
Section

4.3
, the transformed statistics to be ranked are

\displaystyle f(\Phi_{b}^{\rm CP}(X))\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{| E_{b+1} - \hat E( X_{b+1} )|}{\frac{1}{M} \sum_{m=1}^M \| \hat g(X_{b+1}) - \hat g( X^{\rm train}_m(X_{b+1}) ) \|^2 }$}}\;\qquad\;\text{ for }1\leq b\leq n-1\;.

The ranking of
X^{\rm train}
for every input
X_{i}
to find its
M
closest neighbours is the main computational bottleneck in the above approach.

We propose a computationally more efficient DAB variant that, instead of finding
M
closest neighbours to each
X_{i}
, just randomly samples
M
points from the training dataset. Specifically, we consider
\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{b}
defined in
Section

4.3
with the observation-wise augmentation
\phi
given by

\displaystyle\phi(x_{i},e_{i},x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n_{\rm train}})\;=\;(x_{i},e_{i},x^{\prime}_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{\pi(n_{\rm train})})\;,

where
\pi
is a uniform permutation of the index set
\{1,\ldots,n_{\rm train}\}
. In particular,
\pi
removes any sorting in
(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n_{\rm train}})
. The corresponding statistic can be expressed as

\displaystyle f(\Phi_{bj}^{\rm DAB}(X))\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{| E_{b+1} - \hat E( X_{b+1} )|}{\frac{1}{M} \sum_{m=1}^M \| \hat g(X_{b+1}) - \hat g( \tilde X^{\rm train}_{\pi_{bj}(m)} ) \|^2 }$}}\;\qquad\;\text{ for }1\leq b\leq n-1\;,1\leq j\leq k\;,

where we have enumerated
X^{\rm train}=(\tilde{X}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{n_{\rm train}})
and taken
\pi_{bj}
’s to be i.i.d. copies of
\pi
. Note that we use
k=1
in this setup to ensure computational efficiency.

Figure

7
(ii) and
Figure

28
report the experiments for comparing the quality of conformal prediction against DAB with
k=1
as a function of the time taken for computation. For a similar coverage level and compute, we see that DAB achieves consistently smaller CI than conformal prediction.
Figure

29
includes an ablation test with varying
k
, which again shows that DAB has similar coverage as conformal prediction but smaller CIs. Note however that for
k>1
, the computational cost is multiplied by
k
.

We also remark that, while this is a molecular dataset with inherent geometric symmetries, the symmetries have already been incorporated in the neural network structure and cannot be used for DAB. We therefore exploit invariance structures in the conformal score function and use DAB to justify the incorporation of additional random transformations on top of conformal prediction.

and with the conformal score function of
[

. Same setup as

(ii), except that we fix the calibration size of
Split-CP
as
n=30
and that of
Split-CP+Randomised Neighbours
as

. The

-axis is now

, the number of additional augmentations on top of the cycling operations in conformal prediction; note that varying
k
does not have an effect on
Split-CP
.

(c) Language model conformal prediction
[
53
]
for the MMLU dataset
[
39
]
with a pretrained Qwen3-14B language model
[
91
]
.
We consider the CP method proposed by
53
applied to the multi-choice question answering dataset MMLU. Let
\mathcal{X}
be the text input space,
\mathcal{Y}
be the set of finite choices, and
\Delta^{|\mathcal{Y}|}
denote the
|\mathcal{Y}|
-dimensional probability simplex. Given a pre-trained large language model
\hat{g}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta^{|\mathcal{Y}|}
and a dataset of question-answer pairs
X=(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\leq n}
, the CP method in
53
amounts to ranking the statistics

\displaystyle f(X)\;=

\displaystyle\;1-\big(\hat{g}(X_{1})\big)_{Y_{1}}

and

\displaystyle f(\Phi_{b}(X))\;=

\displaystyle\;1-\big(\hat{g}(X_{b+1})\big)_{Y_{b+1}}\;\text{ for $1\leq b\leq n-1$\;,}

where
(\hat{g}(X_{1}))_{Y_{1}}
reads out the predicted probability of
\hat{g}
on
X_{1}
at the location of the true label
Y_{1}
.

Our DAB variant of CP is motivated by the observation that the true question-label pair
(X_{i},Y_{i})
is invariant under the permutation of choice numbering, as illustrated in
Figure

30
by renumbering choice
C
as
D
and choice
D
as
C
. We therefore use
\Phi^{\rm DAB}_{b}
defined in
Section

4.3
with the observation-wise augmentation
\phi
given as a uniformly random permutation of the choice numbering, which is a random
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
that affects both the question text and the answer.

Figures

31
and
32
report results with a subset of the MMLU (Massive Multitask Language Understanding) dataset by
39
, which consists of subject-specific multiple choice questions. In more detail, we generate prediction probability vectors by a pretrained Qwen3-14B language model
[
91
]
for MMLU data under the first 6 subjects, namely (i) college computer science, (ii) formal logic, (iii) high school computer science, (iv) computer security, (v) machine learning and (vi) clinical knowledge. For each random trial, we then make
n
random draws of the generated latents and perform conformal prediction for one of the data with the other
n-1
calibration data points.
Figure

31
is for
k=1
, whereas
Figure

32
performs an ablation test with varying
k
; in both setups, there is no visible improvement from DAB compared to conformal prediction. We conjecture that this is because the Qwen3-14B model is already close to being exactly invariant under question choice permutations, and there are not enough additional symmetries to be exploited by DAB.

input:

Any set of Boolean operators that is sufficient to represent all Boolean expressions is said to be complete. Which of the following is NOT complete?

A:
{AND, NOT}

B:
{NOT, OR}

C:
{AND, OR}

D:
{NAND}

target:
